1.4.
Notation

Unless explicitly said otherwise, the group
G
denotes
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
and
\chi(G)
denotes its group of characters. The identity element of
G
is denoted as
0\in G
, and the trivial character as
1\in G*
. In our case, the theory of abelian covers associates a divisor
D_{g}
to each element
g\in G\setminus 0
. For simplicity, we associate the trivial divisor with the identity, so
D_{0}=0
always.

1.5.
Acknowledgments

We thank Long Horizon Research
for providing us with the Sundial platform and the AI tools to proofread the arguments and presentation of this paper.
Patricio Gallardo is partially supported by the National Science Foundation under Grant No. DMS-2316749.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Abelian covers

We recall the description of abelian covers following Pardini
[
21
]
and the extension to singular bases by Alexeev–Pardini
[
28
]
, focusing on the cases relevant to our work.
Throughout,
G
denotes the finite abelian group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
and

f\colon X\to Y
a
G
-cover (i.e. a finite morphism endowed with a faithful

G
-action on
X

such that
Y=X/G
), and
G^{*}:=\operatorname{Hom}(G,\mathbb{C}^{*})
its character group.

In our work, we assume that
Y
is normal, irreducible, and
X
is normal. The
G
-action yields the eigensheaf decomposition

(1)

f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\;=\;\bigoplus_{\chi\in G^{*}}L_{\chi}^{-1},\qquad L_{1}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{Y},

where
1\in G^{*}
is the trivial character and
G
acts on
L_{\chi}^{-1}
via
\chi
.

If
Y
is smooth, or if it’s the smooth locus, then, by purity of the branch locus, the set of ramification points has divisorial image on
Y
. Let
D\subset Y
be the reduced branch divisor and
R\subset X
the reduced ramification divisor. Each irreducible component

T\subset R
has an inertia subgroup
H_{T}\leq G
, namely the subgroup of elements fixing
T
pointwise which in our case it is always isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Moreover, attached to
T
there is a distinguished generator
\psi_{T}\in H_{T}^{*}
describing the local
action in a transversal parameter. Grouping components with the same pair
(H,\psi)
produces a decomposition

R\;=\;\sum_{(H,\psi)}R_{H,\psi},\qquad D\;=\;\sum_{(H,\psi)}D_{H,\psi},\quad\text{with }R_{H,\psi}=f^{-1}(D_{H,\psi})\text{ as reduced divisors.}

We refer to the collection
\{L_{\chi}\}_{\chi\in G^{*}}
together with the reduced effective divisors

\{D_{H,\psi}\}
as the
building data
of the cover. In our case of
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
, the generator of
H_{T}^{*}
is unique, so it is convenient to index the divisors by

g\in G
and write

(2)

D\;=\;\sum_{g\in G}D_{g},\qquad D_{0}=0,

where
D_{g}
is the reduced divisor whose pullback is the corresponding summand of the ramification divisor.

The building data are constrained by the
fundamental relations
.
To state them for our case, fix
g\in G
and
\chi\in G^{*}
such that
g\neq 0
and
\chi\neq 1
, there is a unique integer

0\leq r_{g}^{\chi}\leq 1
such that
\chi(g)=\exp\!\left(\pi i\,r_{g}^{\chi}\right),
that is

\displaystyle r_{g}^{\chi}=\begin{cases}0&\text{$\chi$ acts trivially on $g$, that is }\chi(g)=1\\
1&\text{$\chi$ acts non-trivially on $g$, that is }\chi(g)=-1\end{cases}

For
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in G^{*}
set

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{g}_{\chi,\chi^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}1,&r_{g}^{\chi}+r_{g}^{\chi^{\prime}}\geq 2,\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}1,&\text{Both $\chi$ and $\chi^{\prime}$ acts non-trivially on }g,\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then for all
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in G^{*}
one
has an isomorphism

(3)

L_{\chi}\otimes L_{\chi^{\prime}}\;\simeq\;L_{\chi\chi^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y}\!\left(\sum_{g\in G}\varepsilon^{g}_{\chi,\chi^{\prime}}\,D_{g}\right)\simeq L_{\chi\chi^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y}\!\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\in G\\
\chi(g)=\chi^{\prime}(g)=-1\end{subarray}}D_{g}\right).

If moreover
\operatorname{Pic}(Y)
is torsion-free, we may write (
3
) additively in
\operatorname{Pic}(Y)
as

(4)

\displaystyle L_{\chi}+L_{\chi^{\prime}}\;\equiv\;L_{\chi\chi^{\prime}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\in G\\
\chi(g)=\chi^{\prime}(g)=-1\end{subarray}}D_{g}.

Taking
\chi^{\prime}=\chi
yields the frequently used relation when
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}

(5)

2L_{\chi}\;=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\in G\\
\chi(g)=-1\end{subarray}}D_{g},\qquad\chi\neq 1.

In practice, it suffices to impose (
5
) for a basis
\{\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{s}\}
of
G^{*}
which is viewed as an
\mathbb{F}_{2}
–vector space since the remaining relations follow formally from (
4
).
The collection of equations (
5
) for such a basis is known as
reduced building data
.
Moreover, if
Y
is smooth, irreducible with
H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}^{*})=\mathbb{K}^{*}
and one is given line bundles
\{L_{\chi}\}
and reduced effective divisors

\{D_{g}\}
satisfying (
4
), then there exists a unique, up to isomorphism of
G
–covers, normal
G
–cover
f\colon X\to Y
with such building data, see
[
21
, Proposition
2.1
]
.

We now summarize the adjustments needed when
Y
is only normal. Following
[
28
]
, assume that
Y
is
S_{2}

and regular in codimension one, and that
f\colon X\to Y
is a
G
–cover with
X
an
S_{2}
scheme. A basic extension principle is that
G
–covers over an open subset extend uniquely across codimension
\geq 2
.

Lemma 2.1.1

(
[
28
, Lem. 1.2]
)
.

Let
Y
be an
S_{2}
scheme and let
j\colon Y_{0}\hookrightarrow Y
be an open subset with
\operatorname{codim}(Y\setminus Y_{0})\geq 2
.
If
f_{0}\colon X_{0}\to Y_{0}
is a
G
–cover with
X_{0}

S_{2}
, then there exists a unique
G
–cover
f\colon X\to Y

with
X

S_{2}
whose restriction to
Y_{0}
is
f_{0}
.

Consequently, the formalism above persists with the following replacements (loc. cit.):

(1)

In (
1
), the eigensheaves
L_{\chi}^{-1}
are reflexive sheaves of rank one on
Y

(invertible on the smooth locus of
Y
).

(2)

The divisors
D_{g}
(or more generally
D_{H,\psi}
) are Weil divisors; their restrictions to the smooth locus are Cartier.

(3)

The relations (
4
) still hold, interpreted in the
group of rank-one reflexive sheaves.

(4)

The morphism
f
need not be flat. It is flat precisely when all eigensheaves
L_{\chi}
are line bundles.

(5)

Given a
G
-cover
f:X\to Y
and an irreducible subset
S\subset Y
, we define the inertia subgroup
H_{S}
of
S
to be the subgroup of
G
consisting of the elements that fix
f^{-1}(S)
pointwise, or, equivalently since
G
is abelian, that fix an irreducible component of
f^{-1}(S)
pointwise. The branch locus
D_{f}
of
f
is the set of points of
Y
whose inertia subgroup is not trivial. As mentioned in
[
28
]
, we regard
D_{f}
simply as a set, without giving it a scheme structure. If
f
is flat, then
D_{f}
is a divisor. If
f
is not flat, then the branch locus may have non-divisorial components.

Let
G
be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
and

\{D_{g}\,|\,g\in G,D_{0}=0\}
be the building data of the cover
f:X\rightarrow Y
.
It is well-known that when
Y
is smooth
the canonical bundle of
X
, see
[
21
, Prop 4.2]
, is given by

(6)

K_{X}\;=\;f^{*}\!\left(K_{Y}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{g\in G}D_{g}\right).

When
Y
is normal, we use the next expression given by
[
28
]
and the discussion before
[
1
, Lemma 2.10]
)

Lemma 2.1.2
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
-cover of a deminormal variety . Let
E
be a prime divisor of Y, set

\displaystyle a_{E}=\begin{cases}0&\text{
$f$ is generically \'{e}tale over $E$ or $E$ is contained in the double locus
}\\
1&\text{
$Y$ is generically smooth along $E$ but $X$ is singular along $f^{-1}(E)$
}\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}&\text{
otherwise,
}\end{cases}

and define the Hurwitz divisor
D_{\operatorname{Hur}}
to be the divisor
\sum_{E}a_{E}E
.
Then, it holds that

(i)

K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier if and only if
K_{Y}+D_{\operatorname{Hur}}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier,

(ii)

If
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier,

K_{X}=f^{*}\left(K_{Y}+D_{\operatorname{Hur}}\right)
,

(iii)

If
Y
is normal, the Hurwitz divisors are given in terms of the building data by

D_{\mathrm{Hur}}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{g\neq 0}D_{g}

3.
Proof of Theorem
1.1.1

We start with setting up the notation and key definitions in Section
3.1
. The calculation of the invariants

K_{X}^{3}
,
\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})
, and
e(X)
for our

\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
-covers is completed in Section
3.2
. We calculate each bound in Section
3.3
.

3.1.
Setup and notation

The first paper to systematically study the geography of threefolds was that of Bruce Hunt (
[
16
]
).
As in that work, we suppose that our threefolds are
\mathbb{Q}
-Gorenstein threefolds of general type, and we focus on their Chern numbers. These Chern numbers can be written in terms of the volume
K_{X}^{3}
, the holomorphic Euler characteristic

\chi:=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})
and the topological Euler characteristic
e(X)
as follows

\displaystyle c_{1}^{3}(X)=-K_{X}^{3},

\displaystyle c_{1}(X)c_{2}(X)=24\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}),

\displaystyle c_{3}(X)=e(X)

Minimal models exist for threefolds, but they are not unique. So, for any
\mathbb{Q}
-Gorenstein threefold
X
of general type, we have the birational equivalence classes
\mathcal{B}(X)
of minimal models of
X
. By work of Kawamata (see
[
16
, Section
7.1.2
]
), there is a well-defined map

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(X)\mapsto\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle X\mapsto\big[-K_{X}^{3},24\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}),e(X)\big]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Q})

such that for a smooth minimal model, it coincides with the map

X\mapsto\big[c_{1}^{3},c_{1}c_{2},c_{3}\big].

We will study the ratios of Chern numbers for the case where

X\rightarrow Y
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
-cover, and the branch locus
D_{g}
satisfies the following hypothesis that prevents certain pathologies such as non-terminal singularities on
X
, which is a needed for defining the above map from
\mathcal{B}(X)
to

\mathbb{P}^{2}(\mathbb{Q})
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

Let
X\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
be a

\mathbb{Z}^{s}_{2}
-cover of a well-formed weighted projective threefold. Let
D_{g}
be the divisorial component (possibly a Weil divisor) of the branch locus with inertia group generated by
g\in G
. We say the branch locus is generic if the following holds:

(1)

All the branch divisors
D_{g}
are well-formed and quasi-smooth.

(2)

All the double intersections
D_{p,g}:=D_{p}\cap D_{g}
are well-formed and quasi-smooth.

(3)

All triple intersections

D_{p,q,g}:=D_{p}\cap D_{q}\cap D_{g}
are smooth and away from the singular locus.

(4)

There are no other intersections, that is
\bigcap_{i=1}^{t}D_{g_{i}}=\emptyset
for all
t\geq 4
.

We start by describing the singularities of the cover
X
when the branch locus is generic as in
Definition

3.1.1
.

Lemma 3.1.2
.

Let
X\rightarrow\mathbb{WP}^{3}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
-cover branching along
generic divisors
D_{g}
. Then, the singularities of
X
are as follows:

(1)

If

y\in\mathbb{WP}^{3}\setminus\text{Sing}(\mathbb{WP}^{3})
. Then,
X
is either smooth over
y
or it has a terminal
\frac{1}{2}(1,1,1)
singularity over
y
only when
y\in D_{p}\cap D_{q}\cap D_{r}
and
p+q+r=0
.

(2)

y\in\text{Sing}(\mathbb{WP}^{3})
, then
X
has singularities either isomorphic to the ones of
\mathbb{WP}^{3}
or a double cover of them, which is also a quotient singularity.

In particular,
X
is terminal if
\mathbb{WP}^{3}
is terminal and the branch locus is away from the singularities of it.

Proof.

Let
y\in\mathbb{WP}^{3}\setminus\text{Sing}(\mathbb{WP}^{3})
.
The hypothesis on our divisors
D_{g}
implies by Pardini’s result
[
21
, Prop 3.1]
that the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
-cover is singular over
y
if and only if

y\in\cap_{h\in I}D_{h}
and the map

\oplus_{h\in I}\langle h\rangle\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
is not an injection. By hypothesis, our divisors are generic, so at most three of them intersect, that is,
|I|\leq 3
.
On the other hand, if
s\geq 2
, then the map
\oplus_{h\in I}\langle h\rangle\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
fails to be injective only for
|I|=3
. After a linear change of coordinates in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
, we can assume that
I=\{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}\}
where either

h_{1}=(1,0,0\cdots,0)
,
h_{2}=(0,1,0\cdots,0)
,
and
h_{3}=(0,0,1\cdots,0)
or

h_{1}=(1,0,0\cdots,0)
,
h_{2}=(0,1,0\cdots,0)
,
and
h_{3}=(1,1,0\cdots,0)
. In the first case, the map

\oplus_{h\in I}\langle g_{i}\rangle\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
is an injection for
s\geq 2
, so
X
is also smooth over
y
. To describe the second case, we observe that the stabilizer group of the point
y
is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}
, so at an open
U_{y}\subset\mathbb{WP}^{3}
, the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
abelian cover factors as

\pi^{-1}({U_{y}})\subset X\rightarrow V_{y}\rightarrow U_{y}.

The map
\pi^{-1}(U_{y})\rightarrow V_{y}
is etale with group
G/\langle h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s-2}
. In contrast,
the map
V_{y}\rightarrow U_{y}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}
-cover. As a consequence, the singularities at
X
over
y
, are isomorphic to the ones that appear in
V_{y}
.
To find the singularities on the bi-double cover

V_{y}\rightarrow U_{y}

with
y\in D_{h_{1}}\cap D_{h_{2}}\cap D_{h_{3}}
, we write the local equation of
V_{y}
over
y
, as in
[
5
, Eq (2.4)]
. Let
w_{i}
with
i\in\{1,2,3\}
be the fiber coordinates of the line bundles
\mathcal{L}_{i}
associated to the bi-double cover, and let
x_{j}
with
j\in\{1,2,3\}
be the local equation of the divisors
D_{h_{j}}
. It holds that

\displaystyle V_{y}=\{(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{A}^{6}\,|\,w_{i}^{2}=x_{j}x_{k},\,x_{k}w_{k}=w_{i}w_{j}\}.

These equations define a Veronese surface
v_{2}(\mathbb{P}^{2})
in
\mathbb{P}^{5}
. Therefore, the germ of the singularity
\left(V_{y},0\right)
is isomorphic to

\left(\text{Cone}\left(v_{2}(\mathbb{P}^{2})\right),0\right)
which is isomorphic to
\frac{1}{2}(1,1,1)
.

Next, we consider the case
y\in\text{Sing}(\mathbb{WP}^{3})
. By our hypotheses
y
is contained in at most one
D_{h}
because
D_{h_{1},h_{2}}
and
D_{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}}
are away from the singular locus of
\mathbb{WP}^{3}
. If
y
is not contained in any
D_{h}
, then the cover is etale over
y
, and
X
has the same singularities than
\mathbb{WP}^{3}
. If
y
is contained in a
D_{h}
, the
(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}
-cover factors as

\pi^{-1}({U_{y}})\subset X\rightarrow V_{y}\rightarrow U_{y}.

The map
\pi^{-1}(U_{y})\rightarrow V_{y}
is etale with group
G/\langle h\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s-1}
, and
V_{y}\rightarrow U_{y}
is a double-cover, and a double cover of a quotient singularity
is a quotient singularities as well.
∎

3.2.
Calculating invariants of threefolds

Following
[
16
, Sec 6-10]
, we focus on the self-intersection of the canonical divisor, the holomorphic Euler characteristic, and the topological Euler characteristic to classify our covers.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

Let
X\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
-cover,
H_{gen}
be the generator of the Picard group
Pic_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{WP}^{3})
and
d_{g}
be the degree of the divisor
D_{g}=d_{g}H_{gen}
where
d_{0}=0
,
and there is at least one
d_{g}>0
.
Then

\displaystyle K_{X}^{3}=\frac{2^{s}}{\prod_{i=0}^{3}a_{i}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{g\in G}d_{g}-\sum_{i=0}^{3}a_{i}\right)^{3}=\frac{2^{s-3}}{\prod_{i=0}^{3}a_{i}}\left(\sum_{g\in G}d_{g}\right)^{3}+O\left(d_{g_{a}}d_{g_{b}}\right)

Proof.

By Lemma
2.1.2
, the canonical self-intersection of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
abelian cover

X\rightarrow\mathbb{WP}^{3}
is equal to

\displaystyle K_{X}^{3}=2^{s}\left(K_{\mathbb{WP}^{3}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{g\in G}D_{g}\right)^{3}

Since
\mathbb{WP}^{3}
is a toric variety, its canonical divisor can be described in terms of the toric boundary, which yields:

\displaystyle K_{\mathbb{WP}^{3}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{g\in G}D_{g}=\left(-\sum_{i=0}^{3}a_{i}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{g\in G}d_{g}\right)H_{gen}

Our result now follows from

(H_{gen})^{3}=\frac{1}{\prod_{i=0}^{3}a_{i}}.

by
[
9
, lemma 12.5.2]

∎

Lemma 3.2.2
.

Let
f:X\to\mathbb{P}(a_{0},\ldots,a_{3})
be a
\mathbb{Z}^{s}_{2}
-cover with notation as in Lemma
3.2.1
. Then,

\displaystyle\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\frac{-1}{48\prod_{i=0}^{3}a_{i}}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\left(\sum_{\chi(g)=-1}d_{g}\right)^{3}+O\left(\left(\sum d_{g})^{2}\right)\right)

Proof.

We start with a direct calculation:

\displaystyle\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})

\displaystyle=\chi\left(f_{*}\left(\mathcal{O}_{X}\right)\right)

since
f
is finite

\displaystyle=\chi\left(\bigoplus_{\chi\in G^{*}}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\left(L_{\chi}^{-1}\right)\right)

by

[
28
, Eq (1)]
and
\chi(L_{1})=0

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\chi(L^{-1}_{\chi})

additivity of
\chi
and
\chi(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{WP}^{3}})=1

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\deg(L_{\chi}^{3})+O\left(\deg(L_{\chi}^{2})\right)

by Hirzebruch–Riemann–Roch

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\left(\sum_{g\,|\,\chi(g)=-1}\frac{1}{2}D_{g}\right)^{3}+O\left(\left(\sum d_{g})^{2}\right)\right)

by Equation (
5
)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{48\prod_{i=0}^{3}a_{i}}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\left(\sum_{g\,|\,\chi(g)=-1}d_{g}\right)^{3}+O\left(\left(\sum d_{g}\right)^{2}\right)

where the last equality follows
by writing
D_{g}=d_{g}H
and using

H_{gen}^{3}=\frac{1}{\prod_{i=0}^{3}a_{i}}.

∎

To describe the Euler characteristic of
X
, we divide
Y
into a disjoint union of strata
S_{i}
such that the number of pre-images is fixed on every strata. Then, we use the additivity of the Euler characteristic to conclude our result. The details are given next.

Lemma 3.2.3
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
cover with branch data equal to
\{D_{g}\,|\,g\in G,\,D_{0}=0\}
.
Assume that the conditions of Definition
3.1.1
hold, and let

D_{p,q,r}:=D_{p}\cap D_{q}\cap D_{r}
and

D_{p,q}:=D_{p}\cap D_{q}
.
Then, it holds that

Y=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{4}S_{i}

where

\displaystyle S_{4}

\displaystyle:=\bigsqcup_{\{(p,q,r)|\,p<q<r\}}D_{p,q,r}

\displaystyle S_{3}:=\bigsqcup_{\{(p,q)|p<q\}}\left(D_{p,q}\setminus\bigcup_{\{r|r\neq p,q\}}D_{p,q,r}\right)

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle:=\bigsqcup_{p}\left(D_{p}\setminus\bigcup_{\{q|q\neq p\}}D_{p,q}\right)

\displaystyle S_{1}:=Y\setminus\bigcup_{p}D_{p}.

and
\dim(S_{4})=0
,
\dim(S_{3})=1
,
\dim(S_{2})=2
, and
\dim(S_{1})=3
.

For the following result, recall that given three different non-zero elements

p,q
and
r
in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
, we
can interpret them as vectors over
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
; and that they are linearly dependent if and only if

p+q+r=0
.

Lemma 3.2.4
.

Let the notation be as in Lemma
3.2.3
.
Then, the following holds:

\displaystyle e(f^{-1}(S_{4}))

\displaystyle=2^{s-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r=0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})+2^{s-3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r\neq 0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})

\displaystyle e\big(f^{-1}(S_{3})\big)

\displaystyle=2^{s-2}\sum_{p<q}\left(e(D_{p,q})-\sum_{r\,|\,r\neq p,q}e(D_{p,q,r})\right)

\displaystyle e\bigl(f^{-1}(S_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle=2^{s-1}\sum_{p}\left(e(D_{p})-\sum_{q\,|\,q\neq p}e(D_{p,q})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q<r\\
q,r\neq p\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})\right)

\displaystyle e(f^{-1}(S_{1}))

\displaystyle=2^{s}\left(e(Y)-\sum_{p}e\left(D_{p}\right)+\sum_{p<q}e\left(D_{p,q}\right)-\sum_{p<q<r}e\left(D_{p,q,r}\right)\right)

Proof.

In the case
S_{4}
, the locus
D_{p,q,r}
is the union of disjoint points. To calculate
e(f^{-1}(S_{4}))
, we need to consider the number of closed points in the preimage of each point in
D_{p,q,r}
. Recall that for each
D_{p}
, the subgroup generated by
p
, that is
\langle p\rangle
, fixes the preimage of
D_{p}
pointwise (the inertia subgroup of
D
). Therefore, the number of preimages
in the intersection
D_{p}\cap D_{q}\cap D_{r}
is equal to
2^{s}/|\langle p,q,r\rangle|
.
By hypothesis
p
,
q
and
r
are all distinct, we have only two options:

p+q+s=0
and
p+q+s\neq 0
.
In the first case,
p+q+r=0
, then the group generated by
\langle p,q,r\rangle
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}
. In contrast, when
p+q+r\neq 0
. Then, the group generated by
\langle p,q,r\rangle
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}
. Now, let’s talk about the number of pre-images. In the case
p+q+r=0
, we have
2^{s}/2^{2}=2^{s-2}
. In the other case, we have
2^{s-3}
. This implies

\displaystyle e\big(f^{-1}(S_{4})\big)

\displaystyle=2^{s-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
r+q+r=0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})+2^{s-3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r\neq 0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})

For the next case, let’s recall that

\displaystyle S_{3}=\bigsqcup_{p<q}U_{p,q},

\displaystyle U_{p,q}:=D_{p,q}\setminus\bigcup_{r\,|\,r\neq p,q}D_{p,q,r}

On
U_{p,q}
the fiber cardinality is
2^{\,s-2}
,
and by the inclusion-exclusion principle, we have:

\displaystyle e\big(f^{-1}(S_{3})\big)=2^{s-2}\sum_{p<q}e(U_{p,q}),

\displaystyle e(D_{p,q})=e\left(U_{p,q}\right)+e\left(\bigcup_{r\,|\,r\neq p,q}D_{p,q,r}\right)

Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle e\big(f^{-1}(S_{3})\big)=2^{s-2}\sum_{p<q}\left(e(D_{p,q})-\sum_{r\,|\,r\neq p,q}e(D_{p,q,r})\right)

For the case
S_{2}
, we recall that over a point of

U_{p}:=D_{p}\setminus\bigcup_{q\,|\,q\neq p}D_{p,q}

the fiber cardinality is constant and equal to
2^{\,s-1}
. By the inclusion–exclusion principle and the fact that
U_{p}\cap U_{q}=\emptyset
for all
p\neq q
, it holds that

\displaystyle e\bigl(f^{-1}(S_{2})\bigr)=\sum_{p}2^{\,s-1}\,e(U_{p}).

\displaystyle e(U_{p})=e(D_{p})-e\left(\bigcup_{q\,|\,p\neq q}D_{p,q}\right).

In general,
the inclusion-exclusion principle implies

\displaystyle e\left(\bigcup_{q\,|\,p\neq q}D_{p,q}\right)=\sum_{q\,|\,q\neq p}e(D_{p,q})-\sum_{q<r}e(D_{p,q}\cap D_{p,r})+\sum_{q<r<s}e(D_{p,q}\cap D_{p,r}\cap D_{p,s})-\cdots

However,
D_{p,q}\cap D_{p,r}\cap D_{p,s}=D_{p,q,r,s}
which by hypothesis is empty. Therefore

\displaystyle e\left(\bigcup_{q\,|\,p\neq q}D_{p,q}\right)=\sum_{q\,|\,q\neq p}e(D_{p,q})-\sum_{q<r}e(D_{p,q}\cap D_{p,r})=\sum_{q\,|\,q\neq p}e(D_{p,q})-\sum_{q<r\,|\,p\neq q,r}e(D_{p,q,r})

which implies

\displaystyle e(U_{p})=e(D_{p})-\sum_{q\,|\,q\neq p}e(D_{p,q})+\sum_{q<r\,|\,p\neq q,r}e(D_{p,q,r})

Finally, for the case
S_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle e(f^{-1}(S_{1}))

\displaystyle=2^{s}\left(e\left(Y\right)-e\left(\bigcup_{p}D_{p}\right)\right)

where

\displaystyle e\left(\bigcup_{p}D_{p}\right)=\sum_{p}e\left(D_{p}\right)-\sum_{p<q}e\left(D_{p}\cap D_{q}\right)+\sum_{p<q<r}e\left(D_{p}\cap D_{q}\cap D_{r}\right)

so our result follows.
∎

Lemma 3.2.5
.

Let the notation be as in Lemma
3.2.3
, then

\displaystyle e(X)

\displaystyle=2^{s}e(Y)-2^{s-1}\sum_{p}e(D_{p})+2^{s-2}\sum_{p<q}e(D_{p,q})-2^{s-3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r\neq 0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})

Proof.

Since the loci
S_{i}
are disjoint, it holds that

e(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{4}e(f^{-1}(S_{i}))
.
Our first observation is that

\sum_{p<q}\left(e(D_{p,q})-\sum_{r\,|\,r\neq p,q}e(D_{p,q,r})\right)=\sum_{p<q}\left(e(D_{p,q})-3\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\,|p<q<r\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})\right)

because the term
D_{i,j,k}
with
i<j<k
appears three times in the sum:
p=i,q=j,r=k
, and
p=i,q=k,r=j
, and
p=j,q=k,r=i
. Therefore

e\big(f^{-1}(S_{3})\big)=2^{s-2}\sum_{p<q}e(D_{p,q})-3(2^{s-2})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})

Similarly, we rewrite
e\big(f^{-1}(S_{2})\big)
as follows:

\displaystyle e\big(f^{-1}(S_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=2^{s-1}\sum_{p}\left(e(D_{p})-\sum_{q\,|\,q\neq p}e(D_{p,q})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q<r\\
q,r\neq p\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})\right)

\displaystyle=2^{s-1}\left(\sum_{p}e(D_{p})-2\sum_{p<q}e(D_{p,q})+\sum_{p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q<r\\
q,r\neq p\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})\right)

\displaystyle=2^{s-1}\left(\sum_{p}e(D_{p})-2\sum_{p<q}e(D_{p,q})+3\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})\right)

which implies

\displaystyle e\bigl(f^{-1}(S_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle=2^{s-1}\sum_{p}e(D_{p})-2^{s}\sum_{p<q}e(D_{p,q})+3(2^{s-1})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})

By using the previous formulas within this proof and Lemma
3.2.4
and

e(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{4}e(f^{-1}(S_{i}))
, we obtain

\displaystyle e(X)

\displaystyle=2^{s-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r=0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})+2^{s-3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r\neq 0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})

\displaystyle\quad+2^{s-2}\sum_{p<q}e(D_{p,q})-3(2^{s-2})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})

\displaystyle\quad+2^{s-1}\sum_{p}e(D_{p})-2^{s}\sum_{p<q}e(D_{p,q})+3(2^{s-1})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})

\displaystyle\quad+2^{s}e(Y)-2^{s}\sum_{p}e\left(D_{p}\right)+2^{s}\sum_{p<q}e\left(D_{p,q}\right)-2^{s}\sum_{p<q<r}e\left(D_{p,q,r}\right)

Our task is now to simplify this expression.

\displaystyle e(X)

\displaystyle=2^{s}e(Y)+\left(2^{s-1}-2^{s}\right)\sum_{p}e(D_{p})+\left(2^{s}-2^{s}+2^{s-2}\right)\sum_{p<q}e(D_{p,q})

\displaystyle\quad+\left(3(2^{s-1})-2^{s}-3(2^{s-2})\right)\sum_{p<q<r}e(D_{p,q,r})

\displaystyle\quad+2^{s-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r=0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})+2^{s-3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r\neq 0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})

\displaystyle=2^{s}e(Y)-2^{s-1}\sum_{p}e(D_{p})+2^{s-2}\sum_{p<q}e(D_{p,q})-2^{s-2}\sum_{p<q<r}e(D_{p,q,r})

\displaystyle\quad+2^{s-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r=0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})+2^{s-3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r\neq 0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r}).

We observe that

2^{s-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r\neq 0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})=2^{s-3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r\neq 0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})+2^{s-3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r\neq 0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})

Therefore,

\displaystyle 2^{s-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r=0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})+2^{s-3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r\neq 0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})=2^{s-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})-2^{s-3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r\neq 0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})

and we obtain

\displaystyle e(X)

\displaystyle=2^{s}e(Y)-2^{s-1}\sum_{p}e(D_{p})+2^{s-2}\sum_{p<q}e(D_{p,q})-2^{s-3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<r\\
p+q+r\neq 0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,r})

So our result follows.
∎

Lemma 3.2.6
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
be a generic and flat
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
-cover, see Definition
3.1.1
, and denote

A:=a_{0}a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}
and
A_{\Sigma}:=a_{0}+a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}
. Then

\displaystyle e(D_{p,q,r})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{d_{p}d_{q}d_{r}}{A}

\displaystyle e(D_{p,q})

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{p}d_{q}}{A}\,(A_{\Sigma}-d_{p}-d_{q})

\displaystyle e(D_{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{p}}{A}\left(d_{p}^{2}-d_{p}\sum_{i=0}^{i=3}a_{i}+\sum_{i<j}a_{i}a_{j}\right)-\sum_{y\in Sing(D_{p})}\left(1-\frac{1}{|G_{y}|}\right)

where
|G_{y}|
is the order of the quotient singularity supported at
y
.

Proof.

Let
Y:=\mathbb{P}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
,
by
[
17
, Sec I.2.12]
, there is a finite map
g_{cv}:\mathbb{P}^{3}\to Y
of degree
a_{0}a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}
. Since
g_{cv}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}(d)=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{3}}(d)
, we have
D_{p}D_{q}D_{r}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{a_{0}a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}}d_{p}d_{q}d_{r}
. By hypothesis,
D_{p},D_{q},D_{r}
intersect transversely and are away from the singular locus, then we have
D_{p}D_{q}D_{r}
is equal to the number of points the hypersurfaces
D_{p}
,
D_{q}
and
D_{r}
intersect. Hence

(7)

e(D_{p,q,r})=e(D_{p}\cap D_{q}\cap D_{r})=\displaystyle\frac{d_{p}d_{q}d_{r}}{a_{0}a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}}

We consider the following case. By hypothesis,
D_{p,q}
is well-formed and quasi-smooth. By
[
17
, I.3.9]
,
D_{p,q}
does not contain a codimension
3
singular stratum of
\mathbb{P}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
. Therefore, any possible singularity of
D_{p,q}
is away from the singular locus of the weighted projective space, and quasi-smoothness implies
D_{p,q}
is smooth.
Since
Y
is a well–formed weighted projective threefold, it holds that
K_{Y}\equiv-A_{\Sigma}H
and

H^{3}=1/A
where
H
is the generator of
\operatorname{Pic}_{\mathbb{Q}}(Y)
.
By construction
D_{p,q}
is a smooth complete–intersection of the divisors
D_{p}=d_{p}H
and
D_{q}=d_{q}H
. Therefore, its genus is given by

2g(D_{p,q})-2=\deg\!\left((K_{Y}+d_{p}H+d_{q}H)\cdot d_{p}H\cdot d_{q}H\right)=\left(-A_{\Sigma}d_{p}d_{q}+d_{p}^{2}d_{q}+d_{q}^{2}d_{p}\right)H^{3}

and the result follows from
e(D_{p,q})=2-2g(D_{p,q})
.

To calculate
e(D_{p})
, we follow the strategy used in

[
4
, Section A.2]
.

D_{p}
is a surface with isolated cyclic singularities of the form
(D_{p},y)\cong\left(\mathbb{C}^{2}/G_{y},0\right)
.
Associated to
D_{p}
, we have the so called
orbifold Euler characteristic
e_{orb}(D_{p})
, see
[
15
]
, which is the coefficient of
h^{2}
in the series expansion of

g(h):=\frac{d_{p}}{A}\left(\frac{\prod_{i=0}^{3}(1+a_{i}h)}{1+d_{p}h}\right).

A direct calculation shows that

\displaystyle\prod_{i=0}^{3}(1+a_{i}h)

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{i=0}^{3}a_{i}h+\sum_{i<j}a_{i}a_{j}h^{2}+\mathcal{O}(h^{3})

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+d_{p}h}

\displaystyle=1-d_{p}h+d_{p}^{2}h^{2}+\mathcal{O}(h^{3})

Therefore, by calculating the coefficient of
h^{2}
in
g(h)
, we have

\displaystyle e_{orb}(D_{p})=\frac{d_{p}}{A}\left(d_{p}^{2}-d_{p}\sum_{i=0}^{3}a_{i}+\sum_{i<j}a_{i}a_{j}\right).

Now we use
[
3
, Sec 2.11-2.14]
that relates the orbifold Euler characteristic with the standard one:

\displaystyle e_{orb}(D_{p})=e(D_{p})+\sum_{y\in\text{Sing}(D_{p})}\frac{|G_{y}|-1}{|G_{y}|}

to conclude our results.
∎

Proposition 3.2.7
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
be a generic and flat
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
-cover with branch data

D_{g}=d_{g}H
for
g\in G
, see Definition
3.1.1
.
Then,

\displaystyle e(X)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2^{s-3}}{a_{0}a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}}\left(\sum_{p}4d_{p}^{3}+2\sum_{p<q}d_{p}d_{q}(d_{p}+d_{q})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<h\\
p+q+h\neq 0\end{subarray}}d_{p}d_{q}d_{h}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\sum d_{i}\right)^{2}\right)

Proof.

To obtain the expression for
e(X)
, we replace the expressions from Lemma
3.2.6
into Lemma
3.2.5
, and then we identify the monomials on
d_{h}
raised to the highest powers. We provide the details next.
Let’s recall that we denote

A:=a_{0}a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}
and
A_{\Sigma}:=\sum a_{i}
. The expressions from Lemma
3.2.6
and the fact that the index of the singularities only depends on
a_{i}
and not on the degrees
d_{h}
imply the following:

\displaystyle e(D_{p,q,h})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{d_{p}d_{q}d_{h}}{A}

\displaystyle e(D_{p,q})

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{p}d_{q}}{A}\,(A_{\Sigma}-d_{p}-d_{q})=-\frac{d_{p}d_{q}}{A}\,(d_{p}+d_{q})+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\sum d_{i}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle e(D_{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{p}}{A}\left(d_{p}^{2}-d_{p}\sum_{i=0}^{3}a_{i}+\sum_{i<j}a_{i}a_{j}\right)-\sum_{y\in Sing(D_{p})}\left(1-\frac{1}{|G_{y}|}\right)=\frac{d_{p}^{3}}{A}+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\sum d_{i}\right)^{2}\right)

We now replace in the expression of
e(X)
to obtain

\displaystyle e(X)=

\displaystyle\;2^{s}e(Y)-2^{s-1}\sum_{p}e(D_{p})+2^{s-2}\sum_{p<q}e(D_{p,q})-2^{s-3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<h\\
p+q+h\neq 0\end{subarray}}e(D_{p,q,h})

\displaystyle=\;-2^{s-1}\sum_{p}\frac{d_{p}^{3}}{A}-2^{s-2}\sum_{p<q}\frac{d_{p}d_{q}}{A}\,(d_{p}+d_{q})-2^{s-3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<h\\
p+q+h\neq 0\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\frac{d_{p}d_{q}d_{h}}{A}+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\sum d_{i}\right)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2^{s-3}}{A}\left(4\sum_{p}d_{p}^{3}+2\sum_{p<q}d_{p}d_{q}\,(d_{p}+d_{q})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<h\\
p+q+h\neq 0\end{subarray}}\displaystyle d_{p}d_{q}d_{h}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\sum d_{i}\right)^{2}\right)

∎

3.3.
Finding the asymptotics of Chern ratios

A key aspect of our work is that we focus on the ratio among degrees of the branch locus, as defined below, rather than on its particular values

Definition 3.3.1
.

Let
\{D_{g}\;|\;g\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}\}
be building data, then we define its ratios as

\displaystyle r_{g}:=\frac{\deg(D_{g})}{\sum_{h\in G\setminus 0}\deg(D_{h})},

\displaystyle r_{0}:=0

Notice that
0\leq r_{h}\leq 1
for all
h\in G
, and
\sum_{h\in G}r_{h}=1
.

Definition 3.3.2
.

Given
n=2^{s}-1
, let

\mathbf{x}_{g}=(x_{g}\,|\,g\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}_{2}\setminus\{0\})
be a vector indexed by the elements of the group, for example, the degrees of the divisors
D_{g}
.
Let
P:\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
be a function, we denote

P(\mathbf{x}_{g})\sim Q(\mathbf{x}_{g})

if
\lim_{\mathbf{x}_{g}\rightarrow\infty}\displaystyle\frac{P(\mathbf{x}_{g})}{Q(\mathbf{x}_{g})}=1
, where by
{\mathbf{x}_{g}\rightarrow\infty}
, we mean
x_{g}\rightarrow\infty
for at least one
g\in\mathbb{Z}^{s}_{2}\setminus\{0\}

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Let
f:X\to\mathbb{P}(a_{0},\ldots,a_{3})
be a
\mathbb{Z}^{s}_{2}
-cover with notation as in Lemma
3.2.1
. Then,

\displaystyle\frac{\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})}{K_{X}^{3}}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{-1}{3(2^{s+1})}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\left(\sum_{\chi(g)=-1}r_{g}\right)^{3}

with
\sum_{g\in G\setminus 0}r_{g}=1
and
0\leq r_{g}\leq 1
.

Proof.

By Lemmas
3.2.1
and
Lemma
3.2.2

our previous calculation we have

\displaystyle\frac{\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})}{K_{X}^{3}}=-\left(\frac{1}{3(2^{s+1})}\right)\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\left(\sum_{g\,|\,\chi(g)=-1}\frac{d_{g}}{\sum_{g\in G}d_{g}}\right)^{3}

So the statement follows from the expression of
r_{g}
given in Definition
3.3.1
.
∎

Here, we prove the expression for the ratio between
-K_{X}^{3}
and
24\chi\left(\mathcal{O}_{X}\right)
that is given in Theorem
1.1.1
.

Lemma 3.3.4
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
be a generic and flat
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
-cover with branch data

D_{g}
and corresponding ratios
r_{g}
, see Definition
3.3.1
.
Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{e(X)}{K_{X}^{3}}

\displaystyle\sim-\left(\sum_{p}2r_{p}^{3}+2\sum_{p}r_{p}^{2}+\frac{1}{6}\sum_{p,q,h\in Q(s)}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h}\right)

where

Q(s)=\{(p,q,h)\in(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{3s}\;|\;p,q,h\text{ pairwise distinct, and }\;p+q+h\neq 0\}
.

Proof.

We use the expressions from Lemma
3.2.1
and Proposition
3.2.7
. Then, we simplify. The details are given next.
We first use the symmetry of the indices

\displaystyle 2\sum_{p<q}r_{p}r_{q}(r_{p}+r_{q})=\sum_{p\neq q}r_{p}r_{q}(r_{p}+r_{q})

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<h\\
p+q+h\neq 0\end{subarray}}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h}=\frac{1}{6}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q,h\\
p\neq q,p\neq h,q\neq h\\
p+q+h\neq 0\end{subarray}}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h}

to obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{p}4r_{p}^{3}+2\sum_{p<q}r_{p}r_{q}(r_{p}+r_{q})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p<q<h\\
p+q+h\neq 0\end{subarray}}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h}=\sum_{p}4r_{p}^{3}+\sum_{p\neq q}r_{p}r_{q}(r_{p}+r_{q})+\frac{1}{6}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q,h\\
p\neq q,p\neq h,q\neq h\\
p+q+h\neq 0\end{subarray}}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h}

Next, we use that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q\\
p\neq q\end{subarray}}r_{p}^{2}r_{q}=\sum_{p}r_{p}^{2}\sum_{q\,|q\neq p}r_{q}=\sum_{p}r_{p}^{2}(1-r_{p})

implies

\displaystyle\sum_{p\neq q}r_{p}r_{q}(r_{p}+r_{q})=\sum_{p\neq q}r_{p}^{2}r_{q}+\sum_{q\neq p}r_{p}r_{q}^{2}=2\sum_{p}r_{p}^{2}(1-r_{p})=2\sum_{p}r_{p}^{2}-2\sum_{p}r_{p}^{3}

To obtain the last simplification in the statement:

\displaystyle\sum_{p}4r_{p}^{3}+\sum_{p\neq q}r_{p}r_{q}(r_{p}+r_{q})+\frac{1}{6}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q,h\\
p\neq q,p\neq h,q\neq h\\
p+q+h\neq 0\end{subarray}}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p}4r_{p}^{3}+2\sum_{p}r_{p}^{2}(1-r_{p})+\frac{1}{6}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q,g\\
p\neq q,p\neq h,q\neq h\\
p+q+h\neq 0\end{subarray}}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p}2r_{p}^{3}+2\sum_{p}r_{p}^{2}+\frac{1}{6}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q,g\\
p\neq q,p\neq h,q\neq h\\
p+q+h\neq 0\end{subarray}}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h}

∎

3.4.
Upper and lower bounds for the Chern ratios

We need some preliminary results. For each nontrivial character
\chi\in G^{*}\setminus\{1\}
, we define

(8)

\displaystyle A_{\chi}:=\sum_{g|\begin{subarray}{c}\chi(g)=-1\end{subarray}}r_{g}

\displaystyle S_{\chi}:=\sum_{g\in G}\chi(g)\,r_{g}.

and denote for convenience that
r_{0}:=0
and

A_{1}=0
.

Lemma 3.4.1
.

Let
A_{\chi}
and
S_{\chi}
be as in Equation (
8
), then

\displaystyle 2A_{\chi}=1-S_{\chi},

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\neq 1}A_{\chi}=2^{s-1},

\displaystyle A_{\chi}\leq 1.

Moreover, if
s\geq 2
, and
r_{g}>0
for all
g\neq 0
, then
A_{\chi}<1
.

Proof.

We have that
\chi(g)\in\{1,-1\}
because

G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
. Therefore if we denote

B_{\chi}:=\sum_{g|\chi(g)=1}r_{g}

we obtain:

\displaystyle A_{\chi}+B_{\chi}=\sum_{g\in G}r_{g}=1,

\displaystyle S_{\chi}=\sum_{g\,|\,\chi(g)=1}r_{g}+\sum_{g\,|\,\chi(g)=-1}(-1)r_{g}=B_{\chi}-A_{\chi}

We obtain
2A_{\chi}=1-S_{\chi}

and
2B_{\chi}=1+S_{\chi}

by solving these two last equations.

For the second expression, we observe that

\sum_{\chi\neq 1}A_{\chi}=\sum_{\chi\neq 1}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\,|\,\chi(g)=-1\end{subarray}}r_{g}\right)=\sum_{g\in G\setminus 0}\left(r_{g}\#\{\chi\in G^{*}\setminus\{1\}\,|\,\chi(g)=-1\}\right).

Fix
g\neq 0
.
Since
G
is a vector space over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, its character group
G^{*}
has
2^{s}
characters.
For a fixed nonzero
g
, half of the characters satisfy
\chi(g)=-1
and half satisfy
\chi(g)=1
.
The trivial character has
\chi(g)=1
, so among the nontrivial characters there are still exactly
2^{s-1}
with
\chi(g)=-1

and
2^{s-1}-1
with
\chi(g)=1
.
Hence

\#\{\chi\in G^{*}\setminus\{1\}:\chi(g)=-1\}=2^{s-1},

which implies (recall that
r_{0}=0
):

\sum_{\chi\neq 1}A_{\chi}=\sum_{g\in G\setminus 0}r_{g}\cdot 2^{s-1}=2^{s-1}\sum_{g\in G}r_{g}=2^{s-1}.

To prove the inequality, we observe that by construction,
A_{\chi}
is a sum of some of the
r_{g}
, then we have

0\leq A_{\chi}\leq 1.

We now show
A_{\chi}<1
for all
\chi\neq 1
. Suppose for contradiction that
A_{\chi}=1
for some nontrivial
\chi
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\,|\,\chi(g)=-1\end{subarray}}r_{g}=1=\sum_{g\in G}r_{g}

By hypothesis,
r_{g}>0
for all
g\neq 0
, this would force
\chi(g)=-1
for every
g\neq 0
.
By hypothesis
s\geq 2
, so we can find two non-distinct elements
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
with
g_{1}+g_{2}\neq 0
and
\chi(g_{1})=\chi(g_{2})=-1
. Such elements will imply

\chi(g_{1}+g_{2})=\chi(g_{1})\chi(g_{2})=(-1)(-1)=1.

This contradicts the requirement that
\chi(g)=-1
for all
g\neq 0
.
Thus, no such
\chi
exists, and we conclude our inequality.
∎

Lemma 3.4.2
.

Let
s\geq 2
, and define

Q(r):=\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\left(\sum_{g\,|\,\chi(g)=-1}r_{g}\right)^{3}.

Then

\displaystyle\frac{2^{3s-3}}{(2^{s}-1)^{2}}\;\leq Q(r)\;\leq 2^{s-1}.

The maximum is realized at the vertices, that is
r_{h}=1
for a fixed
h
and
r_{g}=0
for all
g\neq h
. The minimum is realized at the barycenter

r_{g}=\frac{1}{2^{s}-1}\quad\text{for all }g\neq 0

Proof.

By definition

Q(r)=\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}A_{\chi}^{3}.

If
r_{g}>0
, then Lemma
3.4.1

implies
A_{\chi}<1
. Since, for
x\in(0,1)
we have
x^{3}<x
and we obtain

Q(r)=\sum_{\chi\neq 1}A_{\chi}^{3}<\sum_{\chi\neq 1}A_{\chi}=2^{s-1}.

When
r_{g}=0
for some
g\in G
, some of the strict inequalities become non-strict, so we have

Q(r)\leq 2^{s-1}
. To reach the equality
we fix some
g_{0}\in G\setminus\{0\}
, we take
r_{g_{0}}=1
and
r_{g}=0
for
g\neq g_{0}
. This would give

A_{\chi}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\chi(g_{0})=-1,\\
0&\text{if }\chi(g_{0})=1,\end{cases}

and since exactly
2^{s-1}
nontrivial characters satisfy
\chi(g_{0})=-1
, we obtain that

Q(r)=2^{s-1}.

Next, we work on the lower bound. Each
A_{\chi}(r)
is an affine linear function of
(r_{g})
, and
the map
x\mapsto x^{3}
is strictly convex on
[0,\infty)
.
Thus
A_{\chi}(r)^{3}
is convex in
r
, and so

Q(r)=\sum_{\chi\neq 1}A_{\chi}(r)^{3}

is a convex function on the simplex

\Delta=\left\{(r_{g})_{g\neq 0}\,|\;r_{g}\geq 0,\ \sum r_{g}=1\right\},

and in particular on its interior defined by
r_{g}>0
. We notice that
Q
is invariant under automorphisms of
G
because

\chi(g)=-1\iff\left(\chi\circ\varphi^{-1}\right)(\varphi(g))=-1.

It follows that
Q(\varphi\cdot r)=Q(r)
, and if

r
is any minimizer of
Q
on
\Delta
, then
\varphi\cdot r
is also a minimizer.

We consider the average

\bar{r}_{g}\coloneqq\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(G)|}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathrm{Aut}(G)}r_{\varphi^{-1}(g)}

whereby construction,
\bar{r}_{g}
is independent of
g\neq 0
because the automorphism group acts transitively on the non-zero elements of
(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}
.
Jensen’s inequality for convex functions applies to
Q
. It implies that if
r_{unf}
is the vector with all entries
equal to
\frac{1}{2^{s}-1}
, then:

Q(r_{unf})\leq\frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(G)|}\sum_{\varphi}Q(\varphi\cdot r)=Q(r),

By Lemma
3.4.1
,
A_{\chi}<1
on the interior of
\Delta
and
x\to x^{3}
is strictly convex within the interior of
[0,1]
. This implies that
Q
is strictly convex on the interior of
\Delta
, so the unique minimizer in the
region
r_{g}>0
is

r_{g}=\frac{1}{2^{s}-1}\quad\forall g\neq 0.

and we have

Q(r)\geq Q(r_{\mathrm{unf}})=\frac{2^{3s-3}}{(2^{s}-1)^{2}},

with
r_{\mathrm{unf}}
the only minimizer in the interior of
\Delta
.
To show the uniqueness of the minimizer, we show that it cannot be on the boundary of
\Delta
. For that, let
\hat{r}
be a minimizer on the boundary. Since all the entries are non-negative. Then,

\frac{\hat{r}+r_{unf}}{2}
is in the interior of
\Delta
and

Q\left(\frac{\hat{r}+r_{unf}}{2}\right)<Q(r_{unf})

which is a contradiction.
∎

We continue with the ratio of the topological Euler characteristic to holomorphic Euler characteristic. First, we need a couple of preliminary lemmas.

Lemma 3.4.3
.

Let

\displaystyle S_{\chi}:=\sum_{h\in G}\chi(h)\,r_{h},

\displaystyle b:=\sum_{h\in G}r_{h}^{2}

\displaystyle T:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q,h\\
p\neq q,p\neq h,q\neq h\\
p+q+h=0\end{subarray}}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h},

where we recall that
r_{0}=0
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}\setminus\{1\}}S_{\chi}

\displaystyle=-1,

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}\setminus\{1\}}S_{\chi}^{2}=2^{s}\,b-1,

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}\setminus\{1\}}S_{\chi}^{3}=2^{s}S_{idp}-1.

where
1
denotes the trivial character on
G
.

Proof.

We first sum
S_{\chi}
over all characters of
G
:

\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}S_{\chi}=\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}\sum_{h\in G}\chi(h)\,r_{h}=\sum_{h\in G}r_{h}\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}\chi(h),

where we have interchanged the order of summation. Recall that for a fixed
g
, it holds that:

\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}\chi(g)=\begin{cases}|G^{\ast}|=|G|=2^{s},&\text{if }g=0,\\[4.0pt]
0,&\text{if }g\neq 0.\end{cases}

Since our sum runs only over
g\in G\setminus\{0\}
, for each such
g\neq 0
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}\chi(g)=0,

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}S_{\chi}=\sum_{h\in G\setminus\{0\}}r_{h}\cdot 0=0

Now we separate the contribution of the trivial character
1\in G^{\ast}
. For this character,

S_{1}=\sum_{h\in G}1\cdot r_{h}=\sum_{h\in G}r_{h}=1.

Therefore,

\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}S_{\chi}=S_{1}+\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}\setminus\{1\}}S_{\chi}=0,

and our first result follows.

Next, we continue with the second identity.
By definition,

S_{\chi}^{2}=\left(\sum_{l\in G}\chi(l)\,r_{l}\right)\left(\sum_{h\in G}\chi(h)\,r_{h}\right)=\sum_{l\in G}\sum_{h\in G}\chi(l)\chi(h)\,r_{g}lr_{h}.

Summing over all
\chi\in G^{\ast}
, interchanging the order of summation and using
\chi(l)\chi(h)=\chi(l+h)
, we obtain

\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}S_{\chi}^{2}=\sum_{l\in G}\sum_{h\in G}r_{l}r_{h}\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}\chi(l)\chi(h)=\sum_{l\in G}\sum_{h\in G}r_{l}r_{h}\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}\chi(l+h)

Since

\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}\chi(l)\chi(h)=\begin{cases}2^{s},&\text{if }l=h,\\
0,&\text{if }l\neq h.\end{cases}

It follows that only the diagonal terms
l=h
contribute, and hence

\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}S_{\chi}^{2}=\sum_{h\in G}r_{h}^{2}\cdot 2^{s}=2^{s}\sum_{h\in G}r_{h}^{2}=2^{s}b.

Therefore,

\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}\setminus\{1\}}S_{\chi}^{2}=\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}S_{\chi}^{2}-S_{1}^{2}=2^{s}b-1.

The last identity is pretty similar. We start with

S_{\chi}^{3}=\left(\sum_{p\in G}\chi(p)\,r_{p}\right)\left(\sum_{q\in G}\chi(q)\,r_{q}\right)\left(\sum_{h\in G}\chi(h)\,r_{h}\right)=\sum_{p\in G}\sum_{q\in G}\sum_{h\in G}\chi(p)\chi(q)\chi(h)\,r_{p}r_{q}r_{h}.

As in the previous cases,

\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}\chi(p)\chi(q)\chi(h)=\begin{cases}2^{s},&\text{if }p+q+h=0,\\[4.0pt]
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Substituting this back, we find

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}S_{\chi}^{3}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q,h\in G\\
p\neq q,p\neq h,q\neq h\\
p+q+h=0\end{subarray}}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h}\cdot 2^{s}=2^{s}T,

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}\setminus\{1\}}S_{\chi}^{3}=\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}}S_{\chi}^{3}-S_{1}^{3}=2^{s}T-1.

∎

Lemma 3.4.4
.

Notation as before, it holds that

(9)

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}\setminus\{1\}}\left(\sum_{h|\chi(h)=-1}r_{h}\right)^{3}

\displaystyle=2^{s-3}\bigl(3b-T+1\bigr).

Proof.

By using the notation and results of Lemma
3.4.1
, for every
non-trivial
\chi\in G^{\ast}
we have

\left(\sum_{h|\chi(h)=-1}r_{h}\right)^{3}=A_{\chi}^{3}=\left(\frac{1-S_{\chi}}{2}\right)^{3}=\frac{1}{8}\bigl(1-3S_{\chi}+3S_{\chi}^{2}-S_{\chi}^{3}\bigr).

We simplify and use Lemma
3.4.3
to obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}\setminus\{1\}}\left(\sum_{h|\chi(h)=-1}r_{h}\right)^{3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}\setminus\{1\}}A_{\chi}^{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\sum_{\chi\in G^{\ast}\setminus\{1\}}\bigl(1-3S_{\chi}+3S_{\chi}^{2}-S_{\chi}^{3}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\left((2^{s}-1)-3(-1)+3(2^{s}b-1)-(2^{s}T-1)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\left(2^{s}+3(2^{s}b)-2^{s}T\right),

and our claim follows.
∎

Lemma 3.4.5
.

Let notation be as before, and define

\displaystyle Q(s)

\displaystyle:=\{(p,q,h)\in(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{3s}\;|\;p,q,h\text{ pairwise distinct, and }\;p+q+h\neq 0\}

\displaystyle E(s)

\displaystyle:=\{(p,q,h)\in(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{3s}\;|\;p,q,h\text{ pairwise distinct, and }\;p+q+h=0\}

together with

\displaystyle a:=\sum_{h\in G}r_{h}^{3}

\displaystyle b:=\sum_{h\in G}r_{h}^{2}

\displaystyle T=\sum_{p,q,h\in E(s)}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h},

\displaystyle S_{idp}=\sum_{p,q,h\in Q(s)}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h},

Then, it holds that

(10)

\displaystyle\frac{e(X)}{24\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})}\sim\frac{14a+9b+1-T}{3(3b-T+1)}

Proof.

By Lemma
3.2.2
and Proposition
3.2.7
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{e(X)}{24\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{-e(X)}{K^{3}}\right)\left(-\frac{K^{3}}{24\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})}\right)

\displaystyle\sim\left(\frac{-e(X)}{K^{3}}\right)\left(\frac{2^{s-2}}{\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\left(\sum_{h\,|\,\chi(h)=-1}r_{h}\right)^{3}}\right)

(11)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{2^{s-2}}{\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\left(\sum_{h\,|\,\chi(h)=-1}r_{h}\right)^{3}}\left(\sum_{p}2r_{p}^{3}+2\sum_{p}r_{p}^{2}+\frac{1}{6}S_{idp}\right)

Next, we show that

(12)

\displaystyle\sum_{p}2r_{p}^{3}\;+\;2\sum_{p}r_{p}^{2}\;+\;\frac{1}{6}S_{idp}\;

\displaystyle=\;\frac{1}{6}\bigl(14a+9b+1-T\bigr)

First, we rewrite

(13)

\displaystyle\sum_{p}2r_{p}^{3}\;+\;2\sum_{p}r_{p}^{2}\;+\;\frac{1}{6}S_{idp}=2a+2b\;+\;\frac{1}{6}S_{idp},

Throughout, all indices lie in

G\setminus\{0\}
, and all triple sums are over ordered triples. First observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{p,q,g}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\sum_{p}r_{p}\Bigr)\Bigl(\sum_{q}r_{q}\Bigr)\Bigl(\sum_{h}r_{h}\Bigr)=1

\displaystyle=\sum_{p}r_{p}^{3}+3\sum_{p,q|p\neq q}r_{p}^{2}r_{q}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q,h\\
p\neq q,p\neq h,q\neq h\\
p+q+h=0\end{subarray}}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q,h\\
p\neq q,p\neq h,q\neq h\\
p+q+h\neq 0\end{subarray}}r_{p}r_{q}r_{h}

\displaystyle=a+3(b-a)+T+S_{idp}

where we used

\sum_{p,q|p\neq q}3r_{p}^{2}r_{q}=3\sum_{p}r_{p}^{2}\!\!\,\sum_{q|q\neq p}r_{q}=3\sum_{p}r_{p}^{2}(1-r_{p})=3\bigl(b-a\bigr).

Replacing

S_{\mathrm{idp}}=1-3b+2a-T

in Equation (
13
) yields equation (
12
). By Lemma
3.4.4
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{e(X)}{24\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})}\sim 2^{s-2}\frac{14a+9b+1-T}{6(2^{s-3})(3b-T+1)}=\frac{14a+9b+1-T}{3(3b-T+1)}

∎

Lemma 3.4.6
.

Let notation be as in Lemma
3.4.5
,
and denote

F(r):=\frac{14a+9b+1-T}{3(3b-T+1)}

Then it holds that

\displaystyle F(r)\leq 2

Proof.

By using Cauchy-Schwarz, we obtain

(14)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2^{s}-1)^{2}}\leq b^{2}\leq a\leq b\leq 1.

Every inequality is sharp at either the barycenter

r_{g}=\frac{1}{2^{s}-1}
or at the vertex

r_{g}=1
for a single
g
.
Since
F(r)
is increasing in
a
, we can write

\frac{14b^{2}+9b+1-T}{3(3b-T+1)}\leq F(r)

To evaluate
F(r)
at a vertex
v
of
\Delta
, say
r_{g_{0}}=1
,
r_{g}=0
for
g\neq g_{0}
, we observe that at point
a=b=1
and
T=0
, therefore

F(r_{g_{0}})=2

Next, we show
F(\mathbf{r})\leq 2
for all our
\mathbf{r}
. This claim is equivalent to

14a+9b+1-T\;\leq\;6(3b-T+1),

which can be written as

(15)

9b-14a+5-5T\;\geq\;0.

We replace the constant
5
using
\left(\sum_{g}r_{g}\right)^{3}=1
. By the decomposition established in Equation (
13
),

1=a+3(b-a)+T+S_{\mathrm{idp}},

so
5=-10a+15b+5T+5S_{\mathrm{idp}}
. Substituting into (
15
) yields

9b-14a+5-5T=24(b-a)+5\,S_{\mathrm{idp}}.

By Equation (
14
) and its definition, both terms are non-negative. Therefore, Equation (
15
) holds and
F(\mathbf{r})\leq 2
.
∎

3.5.
Proof of Theorem
1.1.1
and corollaries

Proof of Theorem
1.1.1
.

We start by the lower bound. For that purpose, we need the following identities:
Given,

\displaystyle a=\sum_{h\in G}r_{h}^{3}

\displaystyle b=\sum_{h\in G}r_{h}^{2}

\displaystyle\Phi:=2^{3-s}Q(r)

Then, it holds that

(16)

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=\frac{-K_{X}^{3}}{24\,\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})}=\frac{2}{\Phi},

\displaystyle x=\frac{e(X)}{24\,\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})}=\frac{14a+6b+\Phi}{3\Phi},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SCI}(x,y)+4

\displaystyle=y(3x+1)=\frac{4(7a+3b+\Phi)}{\Phi^{2}}.

Indeed, by Lemma
3.4.4
,
Q(r)=2^{s-3}(3b-T+1)
, so
\Phi=3b-T+1
and hence
T=3b+1-\Phi
. Since
Q(r)=2^{s-3}\Phi
, the ratio
y
becomes

y=\frac{2^{s-2}}{Q(r)}=\frac{2^{s-2}}{2^{s-3}\Phi}=\frac{2}{\Phi}.

Substituting
T=3b+1-\Phi
into the expression for
x
from Lemma
3.4.5
gives

\displaystyle x=\frac{14a+9b+1-T}{3(3b-T+1)}=\frac{14a+6b+\Phi}{3\Phi},\quad 3x+1=\frac{14a+6b+2\Phi}{\Phi},

Therefore,

y(3x+1)=\frac{2}{\Phi}\cdot\frac{14a+6b+2\Phi}{\Phi}=\frac{2(14a+6b+2\Phi)}{\Phi^{2}}=\frac{4(7a+3b+\Phi)}{\Phi^{2}}.

and our identities are verified.
Our next step is to obtain a lower bound for the function

\displaystyle H(a,b,\Phi):=\frac{4(7a+3b+\Phi)}{\Phi^{2}}

Indeed, we observe that

(17)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial H(a,b,\Phi)}{\partial\Phi}=\frac{-4(14a+6b+\Phi)}{\Phi^{3}}

\displaystyle a\geq 0,\quad b\geq 0,\quad 3b-\Phi+1=T\geq 0

imply
H(a,b,\Phi)
is decreasing with respect to
\Phi
. So
3b+1\geq\Phi
and
a\geq b^{2}
, see Equation (
14
), yield

\displaystyle H(a,b,\Phi)\geq\frac{4(7b^{2}+6b+1)}{(3b+1)^{2}},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial b}\left(\frac{4(7b^{2}+6b+1)}{(3b+1)^{2}}\right)=\frac{-16b}{(1+3b)^{3}}\leq 0,\;\text{ for }b\geq 0

Since, this last function is decreasing with respect to
b
and satisfy
b\leq 1
by Equation (
14
). We obtain

\displaystyle H(a,b,\Phi)\geq\frac{7}{2},

\displaystyle\operatorname{SCI}(x,y)\geq-\frac{1}{2}

a direct calculation shows that the inequality is attained at
r_{h}=1
for some
h
, and
r_{g}=0
for all
g\neq h
.

Next, we focus on the upper bound.
First, we observe that
\Phi\geq(1+b)^{2}
. Indeed
by Lemma
3.4.1
,

\sum_{\chi\neq 1}A_{\chi}=2^{s-1}
and

A_{\chi}=(1-S_{\chi})/2
. Using
Lemma
3.4.3
:

\sum_{\chi\neq 1}A_{\chi}^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}\bigl[(2^{s}-1)+2+(2^{s}b-1)\bigr]=2^{s-2}(1+b).

Applying the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality with

u_{\chi}=A_{\chi}^{1/2}
and
w_{\chi}=A_{\chi}^{3/2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigl(\sum A_{\chi}^{2}\bigr)^{2}\leq\bigl(\sum A_{\chi}\bigr)\bigl(\sum A_{\chi}^{3}\bigr)\Longrightarrow\left(2^{s-2}(1+b)\right)^{2}\leq 2^{s-1}Q(r)=2^{s-1}\left(2^{s-3}\Phi\right)

from which
\Phi\geq(1+b)^{2}
follows.
In Equation (
17
), we learned that

\partial H/\partial\Phi<0
within our domain. By Equation
14
,

b^{2}\leq a\leq b
, therefore

y(3x+1)=\frac{4(7a+3b+\Phi)}{\Phi^{2}}\;\leq\;g(b)\;:=\;\frac{4(b^{2}+12b+1)}{(1+b)^{4}}.

Finally, we show that
g(b)\leq\frac{20}{3}
on
[0,1]
. Since, the inequality
g(b)\leq 20/3
is equivalent to

R(b)=b^{4}+4b^{3}+\frac{27b^{2}}{5}-\frac{16b}{5}+\frac{2}{5},\qquad b\in[0,1].

A direct inspection, or the use of mathematical software, allows us to verify that
R^{\prime}(b)
has a unique real root
b_{0}
within
[0,1]
. This root is the unique minimizer of
R
on
[0,1]
and it satisfies
R(b_{0})>0

Finally, we show the other inequalities in the statement of the theorem. By Lemma
3.2.2
, it holds that

(18)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{-K_{X}^{3}}{24\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})}\right)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{2^{s-2}}{\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\left(\sum_{g\,|\,\chi(g)=-1}r_{g}\right)^{3}}

with
\sum_{g\in G\setminus e}r_{g}=1
and
0\leq r_{g}\leq 1
.
By Lemma

3.4.2
, we have
the upper and lower bounds

\frac{1}{2}\leq\lim_{d_{g}\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{-K_{X}^{3}}{24\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})}\right)\leq 2+\frac{1}{2^{2s-1}}+\frac{1}{2^{s-2}}

The lower bound
\tfrac{1}{2}
is attained in the limit

r_{h}\to 1
for a single
h
, and the upper bound is attained
at the barycenter
r_{g}=1/(2^{s}-1)
for all
g\neq 0
. Similarly, by Lemmas
3.4.5
and
3.4.6
, we have that

\frac{e(X)}{24\,\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})}\;\leq\;2\,.

The lower bound is using by observing that

\frac{7}{2}\leq y(3x+1)
and

0<y\leq y_{max}
and
0<y
imply

\displaystyle\frac{7}{2(3y_{max})}-\frac{1}{3}\leq x,

\displaystyle\text{ which in our case is equal to}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\frac{3(2^{2s-2})-2^{s+1}-1}{3(2^{s}+1)^{2}}\leq x

∎

Next, we discuss Corollary
1.1.2
.
We recall that in
[
16
, Sec 7.2.3-7.2.4]
,
Hunt calculated the Chern numbers of smooth complete intersections in
\mathbb{P}^{N}
. Then, he showed that their accumulation points lie in the curve
y(3x+1)=4
. Moreover, he defined the zone

\displaystyle\text{Zone}(E):=\{(x,y)\,|\,y(3x+1)>4\},

\displaystyle(x,y)=\left(\frac{e(X)}{24\,\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})},\frac{-K_{X}^{3}}{24\,\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})}\right)

and conjectured there are none smooth threefolds with invariants in such a zone. To construct counter-examples to Hunt conjecture, we recall a construction of Pardini and Alexeev.

Theorem 3.5.1

(Pardini-Alexeev)
.

A
(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}
-cover of
\mathbb{P}^{3}
with
s\geq 3
is called
almost uniform
if there exist a fixed
1\neq\chi_{0}\in G^{*}
such that the building data
D_{g}
of the cover satisfies:

\displaystyle\deg(D_{g})=\begin{cases}d_{g}>0&\chi_{0}(g)=-1\\
d_{g}=0&\chi_{0}(g)=1.\end{cases}

If
X
is an almost uniform cover of
\mathbb{P}^{3}
and it is of general type, then
X
is smooth, minimal and all its small deformations are Galois.

Using almost uniform covers of
\mathbb{P}^{3}
, we obtain examples of smooth threefolds of general type within the forbidden zone E.

Corollary 3.5.2
.

If
X(m)\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{3}
is a family of almost uniform
(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}
-covers of
\mathbb{P}^{3}
with
s\geq 3
and branch data
D_{g}(m)
that satisfies the following condition: There exist an
1\neq\chi_{0}\in G^{*}
, and
0\neq g_{0}\in G
with
\chi_{0}(g_{0})=-1
, and a rational
r_{a}
with
\frac{1}{2}\leq r_{a}\leq\frac{68}{100}
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{\deg(D_{g}(m))}{\sum_{h\in G}\deg(D_{h}(m))}=\begin{cases}r_{a}&\text{ if $g=g_{0}$ }\\
\frac{1-r_{a}}{(2^{s-1}-1)}&\text{ if $\chi_{0}(g)=-1$ and $g\neq g_{0}$}\\
0&\text{if $\chi_{0}(g)=1$}\end{cases}

Then, the limit

\displaystyle\lim_{\deg(D_{g})\to\infty}\left(\frac{e(X)}{24\,\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})},\frac{-K_{X}^{3}}{24\,\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})}\right)

is contained in the forbidden Zone E.

Proof.

We first observe that for an almost uniform cover, the term

T=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q,r\text{ distinct}\\
p+q+r=0\end{subarray}}r_{p}r_{q}r_{r}

is zero. Indeed, by construction there is a fixed character
\chi_{0}
such that
r_{g}=0
unless
g\in S:=\{g:\chi_{0}(g)=-1\}
.
Let
p,q,r
be a triple in
T
, then we have

1=\chi_{0}(0)=\chi_{0}(p+q+r)=\chi_{0}(p)\chi_{0}(q)\chi_{0}(r)=(-1)(-1)(-1)=-1

which is a contradiction. So no such triple exists, and
T=0
. The formulas for the invariants in Theorem
1.1.1
now become

y=\frac{1}{3b+1},\qquad x=\frac{14a+9b+1}{3(3b+1)}

where
a=\sum r_{g}^{3}
,
b=\sum r_{g}^{2}
. Elementary algebra yields

\displaystyle y(3x+1)=C(a,b):=\frac{4(7a+6b+1)}{(3b+1)^{2}},

\displaystyle C(a,b)\geq 4\iff 7a\geq 9b^{2}

We recall that
a=\sum_{h\in G}r_{h}^{3}

and

b=\sum_{h\in G}r_{h}^{2}
.
Then, for
m=2^{s-1}
and
t:=r_{a}
, we have

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=t^{3}+(2^{s-1}-1)\left(\frac{(1-t)^{3}}{(2^{s-1}-1)^{3}}\right)

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=t^{2}+(2^{s-1}-1)\left(\frac{(1-t)^{2}}{(2^{s-1}-1)^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle 7a-9b^{2}

\displaystyle:=F(m,t)=\frac{(-9m^{2}t^{4}+7m^{2}t^{3}+22mt^{3}-18mt^{2}-15t^{2}+15t-2)}{(m-1)^{2}}

such that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F(r,m)}{\partial m}=\frac{2(1-t)^{2}(9t^{2}m-11t+2)}{(m-1)^{3}}

so
F(r,m)\geq 0
for
s\geq 2
and
t\geq 0
. This means
F(m,r)
is increasing with respect to
m
and it is enough to consider
s=3
. A direct calculation shows that

F(4,t)=\frac{1}{9}(-144t^{4}+200t^{3}-87t^{2}+15t-2)

is possitive for
\frac{1}{2}\leq t\leq\frac{68}{100}
.
∎

4.
Deformations of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
covers of weighted projective spaces

In this section, our objective is to find some necessary conditions under which, the general deformation of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
cover of a possibly singular scheme
Y
, is once again a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
cover of
Y
. This problem of course has been studied for abelian covers
f:X\to Y
when
X
and
Y
are smooth in
[
21
]
and
f:X\to Y
flat with
Y
normal and
f
locally simple (which implies
X
is smooth) in
[
20
]
. Combining the techniques above with those introduced by Wehler in
[
29
]
and Schlessinger in
[
24
]
, we generalize their results for non-flat abelian covers
f:X\to Y
when both
X
and
Y
are normal and the codimension of the singular locus of
X
is at least three. As a result, we construct examples of moduli components of stable varieties whose general element is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
cover of a weighted projective space (see
Example

4.3.4
). We also give concrete examples of non-flat
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
covers
f:X\to Y
, with
s\geq 4
,
K_{X}
ample, branched along configurations of hyperplane arrangements such that any deformation of
X
is once again a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
cover with the same configuration of the branch locus.

4.1.
Deformation of finite non-flat covers

We start with some preliminaries. Let
f:X\to Y
be a finite morphism and let
f_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\mathcal{O}_{Y}\oplus\mathcal{E}
.

Definition 4.1.1
.

Let
\bf Def(f)
denote the following functor of Artin rings. For an Artin ring
A
,
\bf Def(f)(A)
is the set of isomorphism classes of Cartesian diagrams as follows

where
\mathcal{X}\to\operatorname{Spec}(A)
is a flat morphism. Define
\bf Def^{\prime}(X/Y)
to be the functor of Artin rings where for an Artin ring
A
,
\bf Def^{\prime}(X/Y)(A)
is the set of Cartesian diagrams as above with the additional condition that
\mathcal{X}\to\operatorname{Spec}(A)
defines a locally trivial deformation of
X
.
By
[
25
, Theorem
3.4.8
, Lemma
3.4.7
, Definition
3.4.5
]
,
H^{0}(N_{f}^{\prime})
is the space of first order deformations and
H^{1}(N_{f}^{\prime})
is the space of obstructions for
\bf Def^{\prime}(X/Y)
where
N_{f}^{\prime}
(called the equisingular normal sheaf) is defined by the exact sequence

0\to T_{X}\to f^{*}T_{Y}\to N_{f}^{\prime}\to 0

If
f:X\to Y
is a
G-
cover with
G
abelian, then let
\bf Def^{G}(X/Y)
be the functor, where for any Artin ring
A
, we require the Cartesian diagram in
Definition

4.1.2
, to satisfy the additional condition that the map
F:\mathcal{X}\to Y\times\operatorname{Spec}(A)
is a
G-
cover over
Y\times\operatorname{Spec}(A)
.

Remark 4.1.3
.

Let
X
and
Y
be normal varieties with
H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})=0
. Let
f:X\to Y
be a
G-
cover, where
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
. If
Y
does not have
2
- torsion in
\operatorname{Cl}(Y)
, by
Equation

5
, we know that
X
is determined by a set of divisors
\{D_{H,\psi}\}
, satisfying some fundamental relations in
\operatorname{Cl}(Y)
, where
H
is a cyclic subgroup of
G
and
\psi
is a generator of
H^{*}
. Further, any set of divisors
\{D_{H,\psi}\}
satisfying the set of fundamental relations mentioned in
Equation

5
in
\operatorname{Cl}(Y)
, uniquely determines
G
- cover
f:X\to Y
. Now fix such a
G
-cover given by the data
\{D_{H,\psi}\}
such that
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{Y}\oplus\mathcal{E}
where
\mathcal{E}=\bigoplus_{\chi\in G^{*}\setminus\{1\}}L_{\chi}^{-1}
. Since
H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})=0
, the line bundles
L_{\chi}^{-1}
do not have any non-trivial deformations when
Y
is fixed. Hence the versal deformation space of
\bf Def^{G}(X/Y)
is given by an open set of the smooth scheme

\bigoplus_{(H,\Psi)}H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}(D_{H,\Psi}))

Remark 4.1.4
.

We recall a well-known fact from deformation theory. Let
F
and
G
be two functors of Artin rings and
f:F\to G
be a morphism of functors. If
df:F(k[\epsilon])\to G(k[\epsilon])
is surjective and
F
is less obstructed than
G
, then
f
is smooth (see
[
25
, Proposition
2.3.6
]
). Consequently,
f(B):F(B)\to G(B)
is surjective for every
B
. Further in this situation,
F
is unobstructed if and only if
G
is unobstructed (see
[
25
, Proposition
2.2.5
(ii), (iii)]
).

Remark 4.1.5
.

Let
Y
be a reduced scheme. Then there is a local-global Ext sequence for coherent sheaves
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
as follows:

0\to H^{1}(\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}))\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\to H^{0}(\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}))\to H^{2}(\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}))\to\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})

Set
\mathcal{F}=\Omega_{Y}
and
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{E}
where
\mathcal{E}
is a locally free sheaf on
Y
. Then the above sequence gives

(19)

0\to H^{1}(T_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{E})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{E})\to H^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}^{1}\otimes\mathcal{E})\to H^{2}(T_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{E})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{E})

4.2.
Deformation of non-flat

\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
-covers

Theorem 4.2.1
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a finite cover, with
X
and
Y
reduced and non-singular in codimension one, such that
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{Y}\oplus\mathcal{E}
, where
\mathcal{E}
is a reflexive sheaf on
Y
. Suppose that
H^{1}(\mathcal{H}om(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{E}))=H^{0}(\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{E}))=0
. Then the natural forgetful map of functors
\bf Def(f)\xrightarrow{\pi_{0}}\bf Def(X)
is smooth. Consequently, any deformation of
X
can be realized as a finite cover over a deformation of
Y
.

Proof.

We use
[
29
, Proposition
1.10
]
. Since
f
is finite, we have, by adjunction (see
[
14
, Proposition
5.10
]
), for
i=1,2

\operatorname{Ext}^{i}(Lf^{*}\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\cdot},\mathcal{O}_{X})=\operatorname{Ext}^{i}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\cdot},Rf_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})=\operatorname{Ext}^{i}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\cdot},f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})=\operatorname{Ext}^{i}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\cdot},\mathcal{O}_{Y})\oplus\operatorname{Ext}^{i}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\cdot},\mathcal{E})

Hence, we have to show that the map (which is inclusion in the first coordinate)

\beta_{1}:\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\cdot},\mathcal{O}_{Y})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\cdot},\mathcal{O}_{Y})\oplus\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\cdot},\mathcal{E})

is surjective and the map

\beta_{2}:\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\cdot},\mathcal{O}_{Y})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\cdot},\mathcal{O}_{Y})\oplus\operatorname{Ext}^{2}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\cdot},\mathcal{E})

is injective.

While
\beta_{2}
is always injective,
\beta_{1}
is surjective if and only if

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\cdot},\mathcal{E})=0
. Now there is a Spectral sequence

E_{2}^{p,q}=H^{p}(\mathcal{T}^{q}(Y,\mathcal{E}))\implies T^{p+q}(Y,\mathcal{E})

where
\mathcal{T}^{q}(Y,\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{E}xt^{q}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\cdot},\mathcal{E})
and
T^{q}(Y,\mathcal{E})=\operatorname{Ext}^{q}(\mathcal{L}_{Y}^{\cdot},\mathcal{E})
.
Hence in order to show that
T^{1}(Y,\mathcal{E})=0
, it is enough to show
H^{1}(\mathcal{T}^{0}(Y,\mathcal{E}))=0
and
H^{0}(\mathcal{T}^{1}(Y,\mathcal{E}))=0
.

We verify that
\mathcal{T}^{i}(Y,\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{E}xt^{i}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{E})
for
i=0,1
.
Locally, choose a closed embedding
Y\hookrightarrow P
with
P
smooth, and let

I
be the ideal of
Y
in
P
. The (naive) cotangent complex is the two-term
complex
c_{\bullet}=[I/I^{2}\xrightarrow{d}\Omega_{P}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y}]
in degrees
[-1,0]
,
so
\mathcal{T}^{i}(Y,\mathcal{E})=H^{i}(\mathcal{H}om(c_{\bullet},\mathcal{E}))
vanishes for
i\geq 2
.
Let
N_{0}=\operatorname{Im}(d)
, so that

0\to N_{0}\to\Omega_{P}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y}\to\Omega_{Y}\to 0

is exact, and let
K=\ker(d:I/I^{2}\to\Omega_{P}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y})
.
Since
\Omega_{P}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y}
is locally free, applying
\mathcal{H}om(-,\mathcal{E})

to the short exact sequence above gives

\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{E})=\operatorname{coker}\bigl(\mathcal{H}om(\Omega_{P}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y},\mathcal{E})\to\mathcal{H}om(N_{0},\mathcal{E})\bigr).

On the other hand, by definition,

\mathcal{T}^{1}(Y,\mathcal{E})=\operatorname{coker}\bigl(\mathcal{H}om(\Omega_{P}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{Y},\mathcal{E})\to\mathcal{H}om(I/I^{2},\mathcal{E})\bigr).

The surjection
I/I^{2}\twoheadrightarrow N_{0}
with kernel
K
induces an injection

\mathcal{H}om(N_{0},\mathcal{E})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}om(I/I^{2},\mathcal{E})

and hence a canonical injection

\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{E})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{T}^{1}(Y,\mathcal{E})

whose cokernel is a subsheaf of
\mathcal{H}om(K,\mathcal{E})
.
Since
Y
is reduced,
d
is injective at every generic point of
Y
,
so
K
is a torsion sheaf. Since
\mathcal{E}
is torsion-free,

\mathcal{H}om(K,\mathcal{E})=0
, and therefore

\mathcal{T}^{1}(Y,\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{E})
.
The case
i=0
is immediate:

\mathcal{T}^{0}(Y,\mathcal{E})=\ker(d^{*})=\mathcal{H}om(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{E})
.

∎

Remark 4.2.2
.

Note that if
\mathcal{E}
is a vector bundle,
\mathcal{E}xt^{q}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{E}xt^{q}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{O}_{Y})\otimes\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{T}_{Y}^{q}\otimes\mathcal{E}
. Hence in this case, if
H^{1}(T_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{E})=H^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}^{1}\otimes\mathcal{E})=0
, then the natural forgetful map of functors
\bf Def(f)\xrightarrow{\pi_{0}}\bf Def(X)
is smooth.

In the following proposition, we prove a sufficient condition to ensure the vanishing of
H^{0}(\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega_{X},L))
on a scheme
X
.

Proposition 4.2.3
.

Let
p:Z\to X
be a morphism of geometric local schemes with closed points
z
and
x
respectively. Suppose that
Z
is smooth and that
X=Z/G
, where
G
is a finite group of automorphisms of
Z
, having
z
as the only fixed point. Let
L
be a reflexive sheaf of rank one on
X
which is locally free outside finitely many closed points. If
\operatorname{dim}(Z)\geq 3
and
H_{z}^{2}(Z,T_{Z}\otimes p^{*}L)=0
, then
H^{0}(\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega_{X},L))=0
.

To prove this Proposition, we first prove a lemma which is the analog of
[
24
, Lemma
(2)
]
.

Lemma 4.2.4
.

Let
i:X\hookrightarrow Y
be a closed immersion of geometric local schemes, and
x
is a closed point of
X
. Let
U=X-x
. Let
L
denote a reflexive sheaf of rank one on
X
. Assume

(1)

\operatorname{depth}_{x}X\geq 2

(2)

Y
and
U
are smooth.

Then there is an exact sequence

0\to H^{0}(\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega_{X},L))\to H^{1}(U,T_{X}\otimes L)\to H^{1}(U,i^{*}T_{Y}\otimes L)

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{I}
denote the ideal sheaf of
X
in
Y
. Then we have an exact sequence,

\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{I}^{2}\xrightarrow{d}i^{*}\Omega_{Y}\to\Omega_{X}\to 0

Let
N_{0}
denote the image of
d
. Taking the
\mathcal{H}om(-,L)
, we have the exact sequence,

0\to\mathcal{H}om(\Omega_{X},L)\to\mathcal{H}om(i^{*}\Omega_{Y},L)\to\mathcal{H}om(N_{0},L)\to\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega_{X},L)\to\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(i^{*}\Omega_{Y},L)=0

The last vanishing is due to the fact that
Y
is smooth and hence
\Omega_{Y}
is locally free. Now since
L
is reflexive,
L=\mathcal{H}om(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{X})
. Hence if
\mathcal{F}
is a coherent sheaf on
X
, we have

\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal{F},L)=\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{H}om(L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{X}))=\mathcal{H}om(\mathcal{F}\otimes L^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{X})=(\mathcal{F}\otimes L^{\vee})^{\vee}

Therefore the last exact sequence gives

0\to(\Omega_{X}\otimes L^{\vee})^{\vee}\to(i^{*}\Omega_{Y}\otimes L^{\vee})^{\vee}\to(N_{0}\otimes L^{\vee})^{\vee}\to\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega_{X},L)\to 0

Since the first three sheaves in the exact sequence are duals, by
[
24
, Lemma
(1)
]
,
\operatorname{depth}_{x}\mathcal{F}^{\vee}\geq 2
and
H^{0}(U,\mathcal{F})=H^{0}(X,\mathcal{F})
for the first three sheaves. Since coherent sheaves on Noetherian affine schemes have no higher cohomology, by taking global sections of the last exact sequence, we have

0\to H^{0}(U,(\Omega_{X}\otimes L^{\vee})^{\vee})\to H^{0}(U,(i^{*}\Omega_{Y}\otimes L^{\vee})^{\vee})\to H^{0}(U,(N_{0}\otimes L^{\vee})^{\vee})\to H^{0}(\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega_{X},L))\to 0

Now
U
is smooth. Note that reflexive sheaves of rank one on a smooth scheme are locally free. Hence
L|_{U}
is locally free. Further on
U
, the sheaf
\Omega_{X}|_{U}
is locally free. Consequently,
N_{0}|_{U}
is also locally free. Since duals commute with tensor products on locally free sheaves, we have that

(20)

0\to H^{0}(U,T_{X}\otimes L)\to H^{0}(U,i^{*}T_{Y}\otimes L)\to H^{0}(U,N_{0}^{\vee}\otimes L)\to H^{0}(\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega_{X},L))\to 0

On the other hand, since
\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega_{X},L)
is supported on the complement of
U
, restricting to
U
, we get

(21)

0\to(T_{X}\otimes L)|_{U}\to(i^{*}T_{Y}\otimes L)|_{U}\to(N_{0}^{\vee}\otimes L)|_{U}\to 0

Taking the cohomology of the above sequence, we have

(22)

0\to H^{0}(U,T_{X}\otimes L)\to H^{0}(U,i^{*}T_{Y}\otimes L)\to H^{0}(U,N_{0}^{\vee}\otimes L)\to H^{1}(U,T_{X}\otimes L)\to H^{1}(U,i^{*}T_{Y}\otimes L)

Now, comparing
Equation

20
and
Equation

22
, we have the exact sequence in the statement of the Theorem.

∎

Proof of
Proposition

4.2.3
.
Pick a closed immersion
X\hookrightarrow Y
, where
Y
is smooth. Let
U=X-\{x\}
. Since
\operatorname{dim}_{z}(Z)\geq 3
, we have that
\operatorname{depth}_{x}X\geq\operatorname{dim}_{x}(X)\geq 3
. We show that under this situation,
H^{1}(U,T_{X}\otimes L)=0
. Then the statement follows from
Lemma

4.2.4
. Let
V=Z-\{z\}
, so that
p
induces a map
p:V\to U=V/G
. Consider the sheaf
T_{Z}\otimes p^{*}L
restricted to
V
. Note that
p_{*}(T_{Z}\otimes p^{*}L)=p_{*}T_{Z}\otimes L
. Further
H^{1}(V,T_{Z}\otimes p^{*}L)=H^{1}(U,p_{*}T_{Z}\otimes L)
. By
[
25
, Corollary
3.1.23
(see also Proposition
3.1.22
)]
,
T_{X}|_{U}
is a direct summand of
p_{*}T_{Z}|_{U}
. Hence
H^{1}(U,T_{X}\otimes L)
is a direct summand of
H^{1}(U,p_{*}T_{Z}\otimes L)=H^{1}(V,T_{Z}\otimes p^{*}L)
. So it is enough to show that
H^{1}(V,T_{Z}\otimes p^{*}L)=0
. Now by the exact sequence of local-cohomology, we have

(23)

H^{1}(Z,T_{Z}\otimes p^{*}L)\to H^{1}(V,T_{Z}\otimes p^{*}L)\to H_{z}^{2}(Z,T_{Z}\otimes p^{*}L)

The left hand side term is zero since
Z
is a Noetherian affine scheme and the right hand side term is zero by the hypothesis. Hence
H^{1}(V,T_{Z}\otimes p^{*}L)=0
.

We now give a sufficient criterion for the vanishing of local cohomology groups which will allow us to apply
Proposition

4.2.3
, to weighted projective spaces.

Proposition 4.2.5
.

Let
Z
be a smooth variety of dimension
n
over a field, let
S\subset Z
be a finite
set of closed points, and set
U:=Z\setminus S
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be a locally free
sheaf on
Z
, and let
\mathcal{G}
be a coherent sheaf on
Z
such that

\mathcal{G}|_{U}
is locally free of rank
1
(equivalently,
\mathcal{G}
is generically
rank
1
and locally free outside
S
).
Then for every
z\in Z
one has

H^{i}_{z}(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})=0\qquad\text{for all }\quad 2\leq i\leq n-1.

Proof.

We break the argument into steps. Let us denote
\mathcal{G}_{\operatorname{tor}}:=\operatorname{tors}(\mathcal{G})
and

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{G}_{\operatorname{tor}}
.

(1)

Reduction to the torsion-free quotient.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{\operatorname{tor}}=\operatorname{tors}(\mathcal{G})
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{G}_{\operatorname{tor}}
.
Since
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
is locally free, it has no torsion on
U
, hence

\operatorname{Supp}(\mathcal{G}_{\operatorname{tor}})\subseteq S
. In particular, for every
z\in Z
the stalk

(\mathcal{G}_{\operatorname{tor}})_{z}
has finite length over
\mathcal{O}_{Z,z}
.
Tensoring the short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{G}_{\operatorname{tor}}\to\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to 0

with
\mathcal{F}
(exactness is preserved since
\mathcal{F}
is locally free) yields

0\to\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{\operatorname{tor}}\to\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\to 0.

Fix
z\in Z
and set
R=\mathcal{O}_{Z,z}
and
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{m}_{z}
.
Because
(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{\operatorname{tor}})_{z}\cong\mathcal{F}_{z}\otimes_{R}(\mathcal{G}_{\operatorname{tor}})_{z}
and
\mathcal{F}_{z}
is
free, the module
(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{\operatorname{tor}})_{z}
also has finite length.
For a finite length
R
–module
M
one has
H^{j}_{\mathfrak{m}}(M)=0
for all
j>0

(e.g. by the Čech complex computing local cohomology with respect to generators of
\mathfrak{m}
).
Therefore, applying local cohomology supported at
z
gives isomorphisms

H^{i}_{z}(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})\ \cong\ H^{i}_{z}(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G}^{\prime})\qquad\text{for all }i\geq 1.

In particular, for
2\leq i\leq n-1
it suffices to prove the desired vanishing with

\mathcal{G}
replaced by
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
; thus we may assume
\mathcal{G}
is torsion-free.

(2)

Reduction to the reflexive hull.

Assume from now on that
\mathcal{G}
is torsion-free and that
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
is
locally free of rank
1
.
Consider the natural morphism

\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\vee\vee}.

Since
Z
is smooth (hence normal) and
\mathcal{G}
is torsion-free, this morphism is
injective. Moreover, it is an isomorphism on
U
, because
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
is locally
free (hence reflexive) on
U
. Consequently the cokernel

\mathcal{Q}:=\mathcal{G}^{\vee\vee}/\mathcal{G}

is supported on
Z\setminus U=S
, i.e.
\operatorname{Supp}(\mathcal{Q})\subseteq S
.
In particular,
\mathcal{Q}_{z}
has finite length for every
z\in Z
.
So we have an exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\vee\vee}\to\mathcal{Q}\to 0

(3)

Rank-one reflexive sheaves on a smooth variety are invertible.

Because
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
has rank
1
, the reflexive hull
\mathcal{G}^{\vee\vee}
is a
rank-one reflexive sheaf on
Z
. Since
Z
is smooth, it is locally factorial; hence
every rank-one reflexive sheaf is invertible. Therefore

\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{G}^{\vee\vee}

is a line bundle on
Z
.

(4)

Vanishing of local cohomology in degrees
2,\dots,n-1
.

Tensor the exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{Q}\to 0
with
\mathcal{F}
:

0\to\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{Q}\to 0.

Fix
z\in Z
and apply
H^{i}_{z}(-)
.
Since
(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{Q})_{z}
has finite length, we have

H^{j}_{z}(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{Q})=0
for all
j>0
.
Also,
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}
is locally free, so at the regular local ring

\mathcal{O}_{Z,z}
of dimension
n
one has

H^{i}_{z}(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L})=0
for all
i\neq n
; in particular this holds
for
2\leq i\leq n-1
.
Thus for
2\leq i\leq n-1
the segment of the long exact sequence

H^{i-1}_{z}(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{Q})\to H^{i}_{z}(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})\to H^{i}_{z}(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L})\to H^{i}_{z}(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{Q})

shows that
H^{i}_{z}(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{G})=0
.

∎

Under some additional hypothesis, we would like to strengthen
Theorem

4.2.1
so that every deformation of the abelian cover
X
is once again an abelian cover of
Y
. To do this, we first need a lemma to compute the normal sheaf of a finite map.

Lemma 4.2.6
.

Let
Y
be a normal variety of dimension
n\geq 3
with isolated singularities,

D
a reduced Weil divisor on
Y
, and

j:U:=Y_{\mathrm{reg}}\hookrightarrow Y,\qquad D_{U}:=D\cap U.

Assume that
D_{U}
is smooth. Assume moreover that for every point

y\in\operatorname{Sing}(Y)
such that
D
passes through
y
, there exists a
neighbourhood
W_{y}
of
y
with
W_{y}\cap\operatorname{Sing}(Y)=y
together with a
quotient presentation

f:\widetilde{W}_{y}\to W_{y}\simeq\widetilde{W}_{y}/G_{y},

where
\widetilde{W}_{y}
is smooth affine,
G_{y}
is finite with

\operatorname{char}(k)\nmid|G_{y}|
,

f:\widetilde{W}_{y}-\{f^{-1}(y)\}\to W_{y}-\{y\}
is étale,
and the reduced pullback divisor

D_{y}^{\prime}\subset\widetilde{W}_{y}
is smooth at every point lying over
y
.

Define

T_{Y}(-\log D):=j_{*}T_{U}(-\log D_{U}).

Then the sequence obtained by applying
j_{*}
to the short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow T_{U}(-\log D_{U})\longrightarrow T_{U}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{D_{U}}(D_{U})\longrightarrow 0

is exact on the right, i.e.
R^{1}j_{*}T_{U}(-\log D_{U})=0
,
and hence there is a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow T_{Y}(-\log D)\longrightarrow T_{Y}\longrightarrow j_{*}\mathcal{O}_{D_{U}}(D_{U})\longrightarrow 0.

Proof.

On
U
, since
U
is smooth and
D_{U}
is a smooth divisor,
we have the standard short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow T_{U}(-\log D_{U})\longrightarrow T_{U}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{D_{U}}(D_{U})\longrightarrow 0.

Applying the left exact functor
j_{*}
yields the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow j_{*}T_{U}(-\log D_{U})\longrightarrow j_{*}T_{U}\longrightarrow j_{*}\mathcal{O}_{D_{U}}(D_{U})\longrightarrow R^{1}j_{*}T_{U}(-\log D_{U}).

By definition,
j_{*}T_{U}(-\log D_{U})=T_{Y}(-\log D)
.
Since
Y
is normal of dimension at least
3
with isolated singularities,

\operatorname{Sing}(Y)
has codimension at least
3\geq 2
, and
T_{Y}
is reflexive,
so
j_{*}T_{U}=T_{Y}
.
Therefore, if
R^{1}j_{*}T_{U}(-\log D_{U})=0
, we obtain the claimed
short exact sequence. Since
R^{1}j_{*}T_{U}(-\log D_{U})
is quasi-coherent,
it suffices to show that its stalk at every singular point
y
vanishes,
i.e. that
(R^{1}j_{*}T_{U}(-\log D_{U}))_{y}=0
.

Set
F:=T_{U}(-\log D_{U})
.
Let

u:Y_{y}:=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}\longrightarrow Y

be the canonical localization morphism, and consider the cartesian square

\begin{array}[]{ccc}U_{y}:=Y_{y}\times_{Y}U&\xrightarrow{\ v\ }&U\\
\big\downarrow{j_{y}}&&\big\downarrow{j}\\
Y_{y}&\xrightarrow{\ u\ }&Y.\end{array}

Since
F
is quasi-coherent on
U
, and
j
is quasi-compact and
quasi-separated, it follows that
R^{1}j_{*}F
is quasi-coherent on
Y
.
The morphism
u
is flat, because on an affine neighborhood

V=\operatorname{Spec}A\subset Y
with
y\leftrightarrow\mathfrak{p}
,
the morphism
u
is induced by the localization map

A\to A_{\mathfrak{p}}
, and localization is flat.
Hence flat base change yields an isomorphism

u^{*}R^{1}j_{*}F\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}R^{1}j_{y*}\,v^{*}F.

Since
Y_{y}=\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}
is affine, the global
sections of the higher direct image compute the corresponding cohomology
on the source; thus

H^{0}\bigl(Y_{y},\,R^{1}j_{y*}\,v^{*}F\bigr)\;=\;H^{1}(U_{y},\,v^{*}F).

It remains to identify
\bigl(R^{1}j_{*}F\bigr)_{y}
with

H^{0}(Y_{y},\,u^{*}R^{1}j_{*}F)
. More generally, if
\mathcal{G}
is any
quasi-coherent sheaf on
Y
, then

\mathcal{G}_{y}\;\cong\;H^{0}(Y_{y},\,u^{*}\mathcal{G}).

Indeed, choose an affine neighborhood
V=\operatorname{Spec}A\subset Y
of

y
, and let
\mathfrak{p}\subset A
correspond to
y
. Since

\mathcal{G}
is quasi-coherent, we may write

\mathcal{G}|_{V}\cong\widetilde{M}

for some
A
-module
M
. Then
Y_{y}\cong\operatorname{Spec}A_{\mathfrak{p}}
,
and by the affine description of pullback of quasi-coherent sheaves,

u^{*}\mathcal{G}\cong\widetilde{M_{\mathfrak{p}}}
.
Taking global sections on the affine scheme
Y_{y}
, we obtain

H^{0}(Y_{y},\,u^{*}\mathcal{G})\;=\;M_{\mathfrak{p}}\;=\;\mathcal{G}_{y}.

Applying this to
\mathcal{G}=R^{1}j_{*}F
, we conclude
