\bigl(R^{1}j_{*}F\bigr)_{y}\;\cong\;H^{0}\bigl(Y_{y},\,u^{*}R^{1}j_{*}F\bigr)\;\cong\;H^{0}\bigl(Y_{y},\,R^{1}j_{y*}\,v^{*}F\bigr)\;=\;H^{1}(U_{y},\,v^{*}F).

Therefore

\bigl(R^{1}j_{*}T_{U}(-\log D_{U})\bigr)_{y}\;\cong\;H^{1}\bigl(U_{y},\,T_{U_{y}}(-\log D_{U_{y}})\bigr).

Now we prove

H^{1}\bigl(U_{y},\,T_{U_{y}}(-\log D_{U_{y}})\bigr)=0.

By hypothesis, there is an affine neighbourhood
W_{y}
of
y
in
Y

and a smooth affine
\widetilde{W}_{y}
such that

W_{y}\simeq\widetilde{W}_{y}/G_{y}
at
y
.
Let
\widetilde{Y}_{y}=Y_{y}\times_{Y}\widetilde{W}_{y}
. We get a finite map

\pi_{y}:\widetilde{Y}_{y}\longrightarrow Y_{y},

where
\widetilde{Y}_{y}
is a smooth affine semilocal scheme,

Y_{y}\simeq\widetilde{Y}_{y}/G_{y}
, and the reduced pullback divisor

\widetilde{D}_{Y_{y}}:=(\pi_{y}^{-1}(D_{Y_{y}}))_{\operatorname{red}}

is smooth. Let

Z_{y}:=\pi_{y}^{-1}(y),\qquad\widetilde{U}_{y}:=\widetilde{Y}_{y}\setminus Z_{y}.

Since
\widetilde{W}_{y}-\{f^{-1}(y)\}\to W_{y}-\{y\}
is finite étale,
the restricted morphism

p_{y}:=\pi_{y}|_{\widetilde{U}_{y}}:\widetilde{U}_{y}\longrightarrow U_{y}

is finite étale. Let

\widetilde{D}_{U_{y}}=\widetilde{D}_{Y_{y}}\cap\widetilde{U}_{y}
.
Since
p_{y}
is étale and
\widetilde{D}_{U_{y}}
is the scheme-theoretic
pullback of
D_{U_{y}}
(which is already reduced, as étale morphisms
do not introduce multiplicities), we have

p_{y}^{*}T_{U_{y}}(-\log D_{U_{y}})\;\cong\;T_{\widetilde{U}_{y}}(-\log\widetilde{D}_{U_{y}}).

Moreover, since
\operatorname{char}(k)\nmid|G_{y}|
, taking

G_{y}
-invariants is exact, and therefore

T_{U_{y}}(-\log D_{U_{y}})
is a direct summand of

p_{y*}T_{\widetilde{U}_{y}}(-\log\widetilde{D}_{U_{y}})
.
Hence

H^{1}\bigl(U_{y},\,T_{U_{y}}(-\log D_{U_{y}})\bigr)

is a direct summand of

H^{1}\bigl(U_{y},\,p_{y*}T_{\widetilde{U}_{y}}(-\log\widetilde{D}_{U_{y}})\bigr)\;=\;H^{1}\bigl(\widetilde{U}_{y},\,T_{\widetilde{U}_{y}}(-\log\widetilde{D}_{U_{y}})\bigr),

since
p_{y}
is finite, hence affine.
It is therefore enough to show that

H^{1}\bigl(\widetilde{U}_{y},\,T_{\widetilde{U}_{y}}(-\log\widetilde{D}_{U_{y}})\bigr)=0.

Now
\widetilde{Y}_{y}
is affine semilocal and

\widetilde{U}_{y}=\widetilde{Y}_{y}\setminus Z_{y}
, so by comparing
cohomology of the punctured spectrum with local cohomology we get

H^{1}\bigl(\widetilde{U}_{y},\,T_{\widetilde{U}_{y}}(-\log\widetilde{D}_{U_{y}})\bigr)\;\cong\;H^{2}_{Z_{y}}\bigl(T_{\widetilde{Y}_{y}}(-\log\widetilde{D}_{y})\bigr).

Thus it suffices to prove that

H^{2}_{Z_{y}}\bigl(T_{\widetilde{Y}_{y}}(-\log\widetilde{D}_{y})\bigr)=0.

Since
\widetilde{D}_{y}
is a smooth divisor on the smooth scheme

\widetilde{Y}_{y}
, we have the short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow T_{\widetilde{Y}_{y}}(-\log\widetilde{D}_{y})\longrightarrow T_{\widetilde{Y}_{y}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{D}_{y}}(\widetilde{D}_{y})\longrightarrow 0.

Applying local cohomology with support in
Z_{y}
, it is enough to
show that for every
x\in Z_{y}
,

H^{2}_{x}\bigl(T_{\widetilde{Y}_{y}}\bigr)=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad H^{1}_{x}\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{D}_{y}}(\widetilde{D}_{y})\bigr)=0.

The first vanishing holds because

T_{\widetilde{Y}_{y}}
is locally free on the
smooth scheme
\widetilde{Y}_{y}
of dimension
\geq 3
,
so it has depth at least
3
at every point, hence

H^{i}_{x}(T_{\widetilde{Y}_{y}})=0
for
i<3
.
For the second, if
x\notin\widetilde{D}_{y}
then

\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{D}_{y}}(\widetilde{D}_{y})
is zero near
x

and the vanishing is trivial. If
x\in\widetilde{D}_{y}
, then

\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{D}_{y}}(\widetilde{D}_{y})
is a line bundle on the
smooth divisor
\widetilde{D}_{y}
of dimension at least
2
,
hence it has depth at least
2
as an

\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{Y}_{y},x}
-module, so

H^{1}_{x}(\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{D}_{y}}(\widetilde{D}_{y}))=0
.
Therefore the long exact sequence in local cohomology gives

H^{2}_{Z_{y}}\bigl(T_{\widetilde{Y}_{y}}(-\log\widetilde{D}_{y})\bigr)=0,

and hence

H^{1}\bigl(U_{y},\,T_{U_{y}}(-\log D_{U_{y}})\bigr)=0.

∎

Theorem 4.2.7
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be an abelian cover with
\operatorname{dim}(X)=\operatorname{dim}(Y)\geq 3
, such that
X
and
Y
are normal with isolated quotient singularities. Let
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{Y}\oplus\mathcal{E}
, where
\mathcal{E}=\oplus_{\chi\in G^{*}\setminus\{1\}}L_{\chi}^{-1}
, and each
L_{\chi}
is a reflexive sheaf of rank one which is locally free outside a finite set of points. Suppose that

(1)

H^{1}(T_{Y})=0
and
H^{1}(T_{Y}\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1})=0
for
\chi\in G^{*}-\{e\}
.

(2)

H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})=0
and
H^{1}(L_{\chi}^{-1})=0

\forall\chi

(3)

H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}(D_{H,\Psi})\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1})=0
for
(H,\Psi,\chi)
such that
\chi|_{H}\neq\Psi^{m_{H}-1}
and
\chi\neq 0
,

(4)

The divisors
\{D_{H,\Psi}\}
are normal and
D_{H,\Psi,U}=D_{H,\Psi}\cap U
is smooth.

(5)

for every divisor
D_{H,\Psi}
and for every point

y\in\operatorname{Sing}(Y)
such that
D_{H,\Psi}
passes through
y
, there exists a neighbourhood
W_{y}
of
y
with
W_{y}\cap\operatorname{Sing}(Y)=y
, together with a
quotient presentation

f:\widetilde{W}_{y}\to W_{y}\simeq\widetilde{W}_{y}/G_{y},

where
\widetilde{W}_{y}
is smooth affine,
G_{y}
is finite,
f:\widetilde{W}_{y}-\{f^{-1}(y)\}\to W_{y}-\{y\}
et́ale
and, if
x_{y}\in\widetilde{W}_{y}
denotes a point lying over
y
, then the reduced pullback
divisor
D_{H,\Psi,y}^{\prime}\subset\widetilde{W}_{y}
of
D_{H,\Psi,y}=D_{H,\Psi}\cap W_{y}
is smooth at
x_{y}
.

(6)

Assume that there exists a smooth open set
i:U\hookrightarrow Y
such that
\operatorname{codim}(Y-U)\geq 3
and
f^{-1}(U)\subset X
is smooth. Denote by
D_{H,\Psi,U}=D_{H,\Psi}\cap U
.

Then any deformation of
X
can be realized as an abelian cover over
Y
. Further in this situation
X
is unobstructed and represents a smooth point in its moduli.

Proof.

We will show that the forgetful maps
\bf Def^{G}(X/Y)\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}\bf Def^{\prime}(X/Y)\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}\bf Def(X)
are smooth. We first show that the map
\bf Def^{G}(X/Y)\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}\bf Def^{\prime}(X/Y)
is smooth. Since
\bf Def^{G}(X/Y)
is unobstructed (see
Remark

4.1.3
), it is enough to show that the map
d_{\pi_{2}}
between the spaces of first order deformations is surjective. By
Definition

4.1.2
, the locally trivial first order deformations of the map
X\to Y
with fixed base
Y
is given by
H^{0}(N_{f}^{\prime})
where
N_{f}^{\prime}
(called the equisingular normal sheaf). First we make the following claim.

Claim:

Let
f:X\to Y
be an abelian cover with
X
and
Y
normal with isolated quotient singularities.

(1)

Assume that there exists an smooth open set
i:U\hookrightarrow Y
such that
\operatorname{codim}(Y-U)\geq 3
and
f^{-1}(U)\subset X
is smooth. Denote by
D_{H,\Psi,U}=D_{H,\Psi}\cap U
.

(2)

for every divisor
D_{H,\Psi}
and for every point

y\in\operatorname{Sing}(Y)
such that
D_{H,\Psi}
passes through
y
, there exists a neighbourhood
W_{y}
of
y
with
W_{y}\cap\operatorname{Sing}(Y)=y
, together with a
quotient presentation

f:\widetilde{W}_{y}\to W_{y}\simeq\widetilde{W}_{y}/G_{y},

where
\widetilde{W}_{y}
is smooth affine,
G_{y}
is finite,
f:\widetilde{W}_{y}-\{f^{-1}(y)\}\to W_{y}-\{y\}
et́ale
and, if
x_{y}\in\widetilde{W}_{y}
denotes a point lying over
y
, then the reduced pullback
divisor
D_{H,\Psi,y}^{\prime}\subset\widetilde{W}_{y}
of
D_{H,\Psi,y}=D_{H,\Psi}\cap W_{y}
is smooth at
x_{y}
.

Then

f_{*}N_{f}^{\prime}=\oplus_{\chi\in G^{*}}\oplus_{(H,\Psi)|\chi|_{H}\neq\Psi^{m_{H}-1}}\big(i_{*}\mathcal{O}_{D_{H,\Psi,U}}(D_{H,\Psi,U})\big)\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1}

Proof of Claim:
We have

0\to T_{X}\to f^{*}T_{Y}\to N_{f}^{\prime}\to 0

Pushing forward, we have

0\to f_{*}T_{X}\to T_{Y}\otimes f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\to f_{*}N_{f}^{\prime}\to 0

Note that
f_{*}T_{X}
is reflexive sheaf. Since
\operatorname{codim}(Y-U)\geq 2
, we have that
f_{*}T_{X}=i_{*}f_{*}T_{f^{-1}(U)}
. On the other hand, by
[
21
, Proposition
4.1
page
207
]
,

i_{*}f_{*}T_{f^{-1}(U)}=\bigoplus_{\chi\in G^{*}}i_{*}T_{U}(-\operatorname{log}D_{H,\Psi}|_{U};\chi|_{H}\neq\Psi^{m_{H}-1})\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1}=\bigoplus_{\chi\in G^{*}}T_{Y}(-\operatorname{log}D_{H,\Psi};\chi|_{H}\neq\Psi^{m_{H}-1})\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1}

and the last equality follows by definition of the logarithmic tangent sheaves (see
Lemma

4.2.6
).

So
f_{*}N_{f}^{\prime}
is the quotient of
T_{Y}\otimes f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\oplus_{\chi\in G^{*}}T_{Y}\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1}
by
\oplus_{\chi\in G^{*}}T_{Y}(-\operatorname{log}D_{H,\Psi};\chi|_{H}\neq\Psi^{m_{H}-1})\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1}
. Now the claim follows from
Lemma

4.2.6
since tensoring is right exact.

Now note that for a Weil divisor on
U
, we have the exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{U}\to\mathcal{O}_{U}(D_{U})\to\mathcal{O}_{D_{U}}(D_{U})\to 0

Applying
i_{*}
, we get

0\to\mathcal{O}_{Y}\to\mathcal{O}_{Y}(D)\to i_{*}\big(\mathcal{O}_{D_{U}}(D_{U})\big)\to R^{1}i_{*}\mathcal{O}_{U}

We show that
R^{1}i_{*}\mathcal{O}_{U}=0
. We only need to check that the stalk of the sheaf is
0
at the isolated singularities. The question is local, so we can assume that assume that
Y
is affine with an isolated singularity at
y
and
U=Y-\{y\}
. Note that we have
\big(R^{1}i_{*}\mathcal{O}_{U}\big)_{y}=H_{y}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})

But since the depth of
y
at
Y
is at least
3
, we have
H_{y}^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{Y})=0
. Hence we have an exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{Y}\to\mathcal{O}_{Y}(D)\to i_{*}\big(\mathcal{O}_{D_{U}}(D_{U})\big)\to 0

Now under conditions
(3)
and
(4)
of the Theorem, we have that

H^{0}(N_{f}^{\prime})=\oplus_{(H,\Psi)}H^{0}\big(i_{*}\mathcal{O}_{D_{H,\Psi,U}}(D_{H,\Psi,U})\big)=\oplus_{(H,\Psi)}H^{0}\big(\mathcal{O}_{D_{H,\Psi,U}}(D_{H,\Psi,U})\big)

Hence
d_{\pi_{2}}
surjects by
Remark

4.1.3
. Therefore the map
\bf Def^{G}(X/Y)\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}\bf Def^{\prime}(X/Y)
is smooth. Since
\bf Def^{G}(X/Y)
is unobstructed, we have that
\bf Def^{\prime}(X/Y)
is unobstructed as well.

Now we show that
\bf Def^{\prime}(X/Y)\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}\bf Def(X)
is smooth. Since we have
\bf Def^{\prime}(X/Y)
is unobstructed, we only need to show that the map
d_{\pi_{1}}
at the level of first-order deformations is surjective.

The space of first order deformations of
X
, is given by
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\Omega_{X},\mathcal{O}_{X})
.
By
[
24
, Theorem
3
]
, we have that
H^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{X}^{1})=H^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{Y}^{1})=0
.
Setting
\mathcal{F}=\Omega_{X}
and
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
in the local global Ext sequence, we have

(24)

0\to H^{1}(T_{X})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\Omega_{X},\mathcal{O}_{X})\to H^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{X}^{1})\to H^{2}(T_{X})

Now using the fact that
H^{0}(\mathcal{T}_{X}^{1})=0
, we conclude that
H^{1}(T_{X})=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(\Omega_{X},\mathcal{O}_{X})
.

Therefore the map at the level of first-order deformations induced by the forgetful map
\bf Def^{\prime}(X/Y)\to\bf Def(X)
is obtained by taking the cohomology of the exact sequence.

0\to T_{X}\to f^{*}T_{Y}\to N_{f}^{\prime}\to 0

Taking cohomology we get

H^{0}(N_{f}^{\prime})\to H^{1}(T_{X})\to H^{1}(f^{*}T_{Y})=H^{1}(T_{Y})\oplus H^{1}(T_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{E})

Now the conclusion follows from the assumptions.

So we have shown that the composition of the forgetful maps

\bf Def^{G}(X/Y)\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}\bf Def^{\prime}(X/Y)\xrightarrow{\pi_{1}}\bf Def(X)

is smooth.

∎

4.3.
Applications to
(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}
covers of weighted projective spaces

We start this section with an application of
Theorem

4.2.1
.
The reflexivity condition on the following results are automatically satisfied when
X
is normal.

Corollary 4.3.1
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
cover where
Y=\mathbb{P}(a_{0},...,a_{r})
,
r\geq 3
and
\operatorname{gcd}(a_{i},a_{j})=1
for all
i,j
. Let
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{Y}\oplus\mathcal{E}
, where
\mathcal{E}=\oplus_{\chi\in G^{*}\setminus\{1\}}L_{\chi}^{-1}
and
L_{\chi}
are reflexive sheaves of rank one which are locally free outside a finite set of points. Then the natural forgetful map of functors
\bf Def(f)\to\bf Def(X)
is smooth. Consequently any deformation of
X
can be realized as a finite cover over a deformation of
Y
.

Proof.

First note that if
\Omega_{Y}^{\vee\vee}
denote the double dual of
\Omega_{Y}
, then we have that
\mathcal{H}om(\Omega_{Y},L_{\chi}^{-1})=\mathcal{H}om(\Omega_{Y}^{\vee\vee},L_{\chi}^{-1})
. This is because both of them are reflexive sheaves since
L_{\chi}^{-1}
is a reflexive sheaf (see
[
27
, Lemma
31.12.8
]
) and they agree outside of a locus of codimension two. Now from the Euler exact sequence (see
[
9
, Theorem
8.1.6
]
, we have
H^{1}(\mathcal{H}om(\Omega_{Y}^{\vee\vee},L_{\chi}^{-1}))=0
since
r\geq 3
(see the proof of
H^{1}(T_{Y}\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1})=0
in
Corollary

4.3.2
) . By
Theorem

4.2.1
it remains to show that
H^{0}(\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega_{Y},\mathcal{E}))=0
. Note that by the condition on the weights of
\mathbb{P}(a_{0},...,a_{r})
,
Y
has isolated singularities. Further locally around every singular point
y
,
Y
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{3}/G_{y}
where
G_{y}
is a finite group. Since
\mathcal{L}_{\chi}^{-1}
are reflexive sheaves, we have by
Proposition

4.2.5
,
H_{z}^{2}(T_{\mathbb{A}^{3}}\otimes p^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\chi}^{-1})=0
, where
p:\mathbb{A}^{3}\to\mathbb{A}^{3}/G_{y}
is the quotient map. Now the vanishing follows from
Proposition

4.2.3
.
∎

We now prove the following result as consequence of
Theorem

4.2.7
.

Corollary 4.3.2
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
cover where
Y=\mathbb{P}(a_{0},...,a_{r})
, with
r\geq 3
,
\operatorname{gcd}(a_{i},a_{j})=1
for all
i,j
and
X
has at worst isolated quotient singularities. Let
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{Y}\oplus\mathcal{E}
, where
\mathcal{E}=\oplus_{\chi\in G^{*}\setminus\{1\}}L_{\chi}^{-1}
such that each
L_{\chi}

is a reflexive sheaf of rank one which is locally free outside a finite set of points. Suppose that

(1)

the branch divisors
D_{g}\in|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(d_{g})|
associated to the data of the abelian cover be quasismooth and generic (see
Definition

3.1.1
).

(2)

For each pair
(g,\chi)
such that
\chi\cdot g=0
,
that is
\chi(g)=(-1)^{\chi\cdot g}=1

and
\chi\neq\operatorname{Id}\in G^{*}
,

\displaystyle d_{g}<\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{\chi\cdot g^{\prime}=1}d_{g^{\prime}}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{\chi(g^{\prime})=-1}d_{g^{\prime}},

(3)

\Sigma_{g}d_{g}>2\Sigma_{j=0}^{r}a_{j}

Then any deformation of
X
can be realized as an abelian cover over
Y
. Further, in this situation,
X
is unobstructed and represents a smooth point in its moduli.

Proof.

We apply
Theorem

4.2.7
. First of all, under the conditions on the weights,
\mathbb{P}(a_{0},...,a_{r})
has isolated quotient singularities. By
Lemma

3.1.2
, we have that
X
also has isolated quotient singularities. We have by
[
10
, Section
1.4
]
,
H^{1}(L_{\chi}^{-1})=0
for all
\chi\in G^{*}
.
Further from the dual of the Euler exact sequence (see
[
9
, Theorem
8.1.6
]
we have

0\to\mathcal{O}_{Y}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{O}_{Y}(a_{i})\to T_{Y}\to 0

Now taking the cohomology, we have that
H^{1}(T_{Y})=0
since
r\geq 3
. Now we show that
H^{1}(T_{Y}\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1})=0
for
\chi\in G^{*}-\{0\}
. Tensoring the above sequence by
L_{\chi}^{-1})
, we have a right exact sequence

L_{\chi}^{-1}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{O}_{Y}(a_{i})\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1}\to T_{Y}\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1}\to 0

Now if we denote the kernel and the image of the map
L_{\chi}^{-1}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{O}_{Y}(a_{i})
by
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{I}
respectively, we have two exact sequences

0\to\mathcal{I}\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{O}_{Y}(a_{i})\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1}\to T_{Y}\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1}\to 0

and

0\to\mathcal{K}\to L_{\chi}^{-1}\to\mathcal{I}\to 0

Now note that since
L_{\chi}^{-1}
is a line bundle outside the finitely many singular points of
Y
, we have that
\mathcal{K}
is supported on finitely many points. Now since
H^{2}(L_{\chi}^{-1})=H^{3}(\mathcal{K})=0
, we conclude that
H^{2}(\mathcal{I})=0
. Further since
H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}(a_{i})\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1})=0
, we conclude that
H^{1}(T_{Y}\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1})=0
.

Since
D_{g}
’s are assumed to be quasismooth, they are smooth when restricted to the smooth locus of
\mathbb{P}(a_{0},\cdots,a_{r})
and have isolated quotient singularities and are hence normal. Since
\mathbb{P}(a_{0},\cdots,a_{r})
has isolated singularities, they appear only at the vertices and the chart
(x_{i}\neq 0)
gives an affine chart around the
i
-th vertex which is of the form
\mathbb{A}^{r}/\mathbb{Z}_{a_{i}}
. Since the expression for the weighted projective space is taken to be well-formed, this gives the quotient representation required in condition
(5)
of
Theorem

4.2.7
. Further, quasismoothness forces the reduced pullback of
D_{g}
’s to be smooth in a local chart at a point lying above a singular point of
D_{g}
. Condition
(6)
follows from the fact that both
X
and
Y
have isolated singularities and that
r\geq 3
.
The third condition of
Theorem

4.2.7
, translates to the first condition since by
[
21
, Proposition
2.1
]
and the fact that
\operatorname{Cl}(Y)
does not have
2-
torsion implies,

l_{\chi}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{\chi\cdot g^{\prime}=1}d_{g^{\prime}}

Now by
[
21
, Prop 4.2]
, the canonical divisor of the cover
X
is given by

\displaystyle K_{X}=f^{*}\left(K_{Y}+\sum_{H_{i},\Psi_{i}}\frac{(|H|-1)}{|H|}D_{H_{i},\Psi_{i}}\right)

Therefore, by condition
(3)
of this Corollary,
K_{X}
is ample. Then,
H^{0}(T_{X})=H^{\operatorname{dim}(X)}(\Omega_{X}^{**}\otimes K_{X})=0
by Nakano vanishing theorem.
∎

Before we discuss our applications to moduli theory, let’s recall that a threefold
X
is of general type if
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and big; that is, the linear system
|mK_{X}|
defines a birational map for
m\gg 0
. If
X
is a reduced, projective variety over a field
k
, we say
X
is a stable variety if it has semi-log-canonical singularities,
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, and
K_{X}
is ample (see
[
19
, Definition 1.41]
). We construct stable varieties via abelian covers by using the following well-known result (for instance, see
[
2
, Thm 6.1.5]
).

Lemma 4.3.3
.

Suppose that
f:X\to Y
is a finite cover of
S_{2}
varieties with double crossings in codimension 1,
B_{X}
and
B_{Y}
are
\mathbb{Q}
divisors on
X
,

Y
, and suppose also that, for the canonical divisors, the following formula holds:

K_{X}+B_{X}=f^{*}\left(K_{Y}+B_{Y}\right)

Then, the pair
(X,B_{X})
is stable if and only if
(Y,B_{Y})
is stable.

By the work of Kollár, Shepherd-Barron, and Alexeev, there exists a projective scheme
\overline{M}_{c}
that parametrizes stable threefolds with
K^{3}=c
, see
[
18
]
. In our next application, we construct new moduli components of stable threefolds such that the general member is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
cover of a weighted projective threefold.

Example 4.3.4
.

Let
M>2
be an even positive integer
f:X\to\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,M)
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{4}
cover with branch divisors
D_{g_{0}}\in|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(2)|
and
D_{g}\in|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(M)|
for
g\neq g_{0},g\neq 0
. Then one can check that
f_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\oplus_{\{\chi|\chi(g_{0})=1\}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(-(1+\frac{7}{2}M))\oplus_{\{\chi|\chi(g_{0})=0\}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(-4M)
. Since the pair
(\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,M),D_{g_{0}})
is a stable pair, we have that
X
is stable by
Lemma

4.3.3
. Further,
(1+\frac{7}{2}M)
is not divisible by
M
and hence
f
is a non-flat cover. This example satisfies all the conditions of
Corollary

4.3.2
and hence defines a new component of the moduli of stable threefolds.

Corollary 4.3.5
.

Let
Y=\mathbb{P}(a_{0},...,a_{r})
with
\textrm{gcd}(a_{i},a_{j})=1
for
0\leq i<j\leq r
and
r\geq 3
. Now consider
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
covers branched along either one of the following configurations of hyperplane arrangements.

(1)

s\geq 3
,
d_{g}=1
for
g\in G-\{0\}
and
\Sigma_{j=0}^{r}a_{j}<\displaystyle\frac{2^{s}-1}{2}
.

(2)

s\geq 4
, fix a linear subspace
L\subset G
, with
\operatorname{dim}(L)\geq 2
, where we look at
G
as a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
vector space. Then
d_{g}=1
if and only if
g\in G\setminus L
. In this case assume

\Sigma_{j=0}^{r}a_{j}<\displaystyle\frac{2^{s}-2^{\operatorname{dim}(L)}}{2}

Then for a generic choice of branch divisors
\{D_{g}\in|\mathcal{O}_{Y}(d_{g})|\}_{g\in G-\{0\}}
, such that
D_{g}
is quasismooth for each
g\neq 0
, there exists a
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
cover
f:X\to Y
, with
K_{X}
ample,
X
unobstructed and all deformations of
X
are once again
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
covers of
Y
branched along the same configuration of hyperplane arrangements.

We break the proof of the Corollary into several parts.
Let
V=(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{s}
. We consider functions
h:V\to\{0,1\}
with
h(0)=0
that satisfy:

(1)

Evenness Condition
: For every
g\in V
,

L_{g}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g^{\prime}\in V\\
g\cdot g^{\prime}=1\end{subarray}}h(g^{\prime})\quad\text{is even}.

This condition ensures that if we choose a collection of divisors
\{D_{g}\}_{g\in G^{*}-\{0\}}
such that
D_{g}\in\lvert\mathcal{O}_{Y}(d_{g})\rvert
, where
d_{g}=h(g)
, then the collection
\{D_{g}\}_{g\in G-\{0\}}
satisfies the fundamental relations and consequently, there exists a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
cover
f:X\to Y
branched along
\Sigma_{g}D_{g}
, with the inertia group of
D_{g}
being generated by the cyclic subgroup generated by
g
.

(2)

Deformation Inequality
: For all nonzero
g,g^{\prime}\in V
with
g\cdot g^{\prime}=0
,

h(g)<\frac{1}{2}L_{g^{\prime}}.

These set of inequalities ensure by
Corollary

4.3.2
, that a general deformation of the abelian cover
f:X\to Y
constructed by a collection of divisors
\{D_{g}\in|\mathcal{O}_{Y}(d_{g})|\}
is once again an abelian cover of
Y
.

Lemma 4.3.6
.

The evenness condition is equivalent to the condition that

\sum_{a\in A}a=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{F}_{2}^{s},

where
A=\{g^{\prime}\in V\setminus\{0\}:h(g^{\prime})=1\}
.

Proof.

Note that
L_{g}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g^{\prime}\in V\\
g\cdot g^{\prime}=1\end{subarray}}h(g^{\prime})
. The condition that
L_{g}
is even is equivalent to
L_{g}\equiv 0\pmod{2}
. Now observe:

L_{g}=\sum_{g^{\prime}\in V}(g\cdot g^{\prime})h(g^{\prime})\pmod{2},

because if
g\cdot g^{\prime}=0
, then the term contributes 0 modulo 2, and if
g\cdot g^{\prime}=1
, then it contributes
h(g^{\prime})
modulo 2. So we have:

L_{g}\equiv\sum_{g^{\prime}\in V}(g\cdot g^{\prime})h(g^{\prime})\pmod{2}.

Define the vector

v=\sum_{g^{\prime}\in V}h(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{s}.

Then

\sum_{g^{\prime}\in V}(g\cdot g^{\prime})h(g^{\prime})=g\cdot v.

Thus, the evenness condition becomes:

g\cdot v\equiv 0\pmod{2}\quad\text{for all }g\in V.

Since the dot product is non-degenerate over
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{s}
, this implies
v=0
. Hence,

\sum_{g^{\prime}\in V}h(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}=0.

Given that
h(0)=0
, we have:

\sum_{g^{\prime}\in V\setminus\{0\}}h(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}=0,

which is exactly

\sum_{a\in A}a=0.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 4.3.7
.

Let
V=(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{s}
, and let
h:V\to\{0,1\}
with
h(0)=0
.
Define

A=\{g\in V\setminus\{0\}:h(g)=1\}
. Suppose that for all affine hyperplanes
H
of
V
with
0\notin H
, we have:

|A\cap H|\geq 4.

Then for all nonzero
g,g^{\prime}\in V
with
g\cdot g^{\prime}=0
, we have:

h(g)<\frac{1}{2}L_{g^{\prime}}

Proof.

Let
g,g^{\prime}
be nonzero vectors with
g\cdot g^{\prime}=0
. Let
H_{g^{\prime}}=\{k\in V:g^{\prime}\cdot k=1\}
.
Note that since
h(g)\in\{0,1\}
, we have
L_{g^{\prime}}=|A\cap H_{g^{\prime}}|\geq 4
by assumption. Hence

h(g)\leq 1<\frac{1}{2}L_{g^{\prime}}

∎

Proof.

Proof of
Corollary

4.3.5
.

First note that by
Lemma

4.3.6
and
Lemma

4.3.7
, it is enough to prove the evenness condition and that for all affine hyperplanes
H
of
V
with
0\notin H
,

|A\cap H|\geq 4.

for cases
(1)
and
(2)
. Let us first prove for case
(1)
.

Define
h(g)=1
for all
g\neq 0
, and
h(0)=0
. Let
A=\{g\in V\setminus\{0\}:h(g)=1\}=V\setminus\{0\}
. Then

\sum_{a\in A}a=\sum_{v\in V}v-0.

In
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{s}
, there are
2^{s}
vectors. For each coordinate
i
, exactly half of the vectors (i.e.,
2^{s-1}
) have a 1 in that coordinate. Since
2^{s-1}
is even for
s\geq 2
, the sum modulo 2 in each coordinate is 0. Thus,

\sum_{a\in A}a=0,

so the evenness condition holds.

Let
g,g^{\prime}\in V\setminus\{0\}
with
g\cdot g^{\prime}=0
. Then

L_{g^{\prime}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in V\\
g^{\prime}\cdot v=1\end{subarray}}h(v)=|\{v\in V\setminus\{0\}:g^{\prime}\cdot v=1\}|.

The set
\{v\in V:g^{\prime}\cdot v=1\}
has size
2^{s-1}
. Therefore, we have
L_{g^{\prime}}=2^{s-1}
.

Then

\frac{1}{2}L_{g^{\prime}}=2^{s-2}.

Since
s\geq 3
, we have
2^{s-2}\geq 2
. As
h(g)=1
, the inequality

1<2^{s-2}

holds. Hence, the deformation inequality is satisfied.

Now we prove both the conditions for configuration
(2)
.
Then
|L|=2^{k}
,
|A|=2^{s}-2^{k}
.
We have:

\sum_{a\in A}a=\sum_{v\in V}v-\sum_{l\in L}l.

Since
\sum_{v\in V}v=0
and
\sum_{l\in L}l=0
(for
k\geq 2
),
we get
\sum_{a\in A}a=0
. Thus the first condition is verified.

Now, let
H
be an affine hyperplane with
0\notin H
. Then
H=\{x\in V:u\cdot x=1\}
for some
u\neq 0
. We compute
|A\cap H|=|H|-|L\cap H|=2^{s-1}-|L\cap H|
.

There are two subcases:

Subcase 2.1:
u\in L^{\perp}

Then for all
l\in L
,
u\cdot l=0
, so
L\subseteq H^{0}
(the linear hyperplane
\{x:u\cdot x=0\}
).
Thus
L\cap H=\emptyset
, so
|A\cap H|=2^{s-1}\geq 8\geq 4
.

Subcase 2.2:
u\notin L^{\perp}

Then
\exists l\in L
such that
u\cdot l=1
or
l\in L\cap H
. Further, the restriction
u|_{L}:L\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
is a nonzero linear functional.
Its kernel has dimension
k-1
, so
|L\cap H^{0}|=2^{k-1}
.
Since
H=H^{0}+l
, and
L
is a subspace,
we have
|L\cap H|=2^{k-1}
. Thus
|A\cap H|=2^{s-1}-2^{k-1}
.
We need to show
2^{s-1}-2^{k-1}\geq 4
for
s\geq 4
and
2\leq k\leq s-1
. Since
k\leq s-1
, we have
2^{k-1}\leq 2^{s-2}
, so:

2^{s-1}-2^{k-1}\geq 2^{s-1}-2^{s-2}=2^{s-2}.

For
s\geq 4
,
2^{s-2}\geq 4
. Equality occurs when
s=4
and
k=s-1=3
. Thus in all cases,
|A\cap H|\geq 4
.
∎

Example 4.3.8
.

For
s=4
, we get the following
7
irreducible families of threefolds with ample canonical bundle, which are general in their moduli. A general
X
in this family is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{4}
cover of a weighted projective threefold
\mathbb{WP}^{3}
, where

(1)

\mathbb{WP}^{3}
is either
\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,1)
or
\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,2)
and the divisorial part of the branch locus consists of
12
hyperplanes, each corresponding to a non-zero element of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{4}\setminus L
, where
L
is a subspace of dimension
2
. Different choices of subspaces give the same component.

(a)

The cover
f:X\to\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,1)
coincides with the canonical map of
X
.

(b)

The cover
f:X\to\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,2)
coincides with the bicanonical map of
X
.

When
\mathbb{WP}^{3}=\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,2)
, these are examples of non-flat pluricanonical covers since the pushforward of the structure sheaf decomposes as

f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{\chi\in L^{\perp}\setminus\{1\}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(-4)\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{\chi\in G^{*}\setminus L^{\perp}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(-3)\right)

(2)

\mathbb{WP}
is either
\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,1),\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,2),\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,3),\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,4),\mathbb{P}(1,1,2,3)
and the divisorial part of the branch locus consists of
15
hyperplanes, each corresponding to a non-zero element of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{4}
.
When
\mathbb{WP}^{3}=\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,3)
or
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2,3)
, the above once again are examples of non-flat covers since the pushforward of the structure sheaf decomposes as

f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{\chi\in G^{*}\setminus\{1\}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(-4)\right)

5.
Pluricanonical
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
covers of weighted projective spaces

Let
X
be a normal variety. If
\mathcal{O}_{X}(mK_{X})
is an ample and base point line bundle then we call the morphism given by the complete linear series of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(mK_{X})
the
m
-canonical morphism of
X
. The main theme of this section is the proof of Theorem
1.3.1
which classifies
m
-canonical
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
covers of weighted projective threefolds. If
f:X\to Y
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
cover then, we say that
f
is an
m
-canonical cover if
f
fits into a diagram as follows for some
m\geq 1
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{|mK_{X}|}}

The strategy is to start with a numerical criterion for a
Z_{2}^{s}
-cover to be the
m
-canonical morphism and then, to classify the branch data that satisfy such a criterion.

Proposition 5.0.1
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
cover where
Y=\mathbb{P}(a_{0},...,a_{r})
. Let
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{Y}\oplus\mathcal{E}
, where
\mathcal{E}=\oplus_{\chi\in G^{*}\setminus\{1\}}L_{\chi}^{-1}
, where
L_{\chi}^{-1}
is a reflexive sheaf of rank one for all
\chi\in G^{*}
. Further let the divisors associated to the data of the abelian cover be
\{D_{g}\in|\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(d_{g})|\}_{g\in G-\{0\}}
. Let
m\geq 1
be an integer. Then
\mathcal{O}_{X}(mK_{X})
is an ample and base point free line bundle and
f
is the
m-
canonical morphism of
X
if and only if

(1)

\forall\chi\in G^{*}-\{0\}
,

h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}(\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}m\Sigma_{g\in G-\{0\}}d_{g}-m\Sigma_{j}a_{j}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{g\mid\chi\cdot g=1}d_{g}))=0

This happens in particular if
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}m\Sigma_{g\in G-\{0\}}d_{g}-m\Sigma_{j}a_{j}<\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{g\mid\chi\cdot g=1}d_{g}
for all
\chi\in G^{*}-\{0\}

(2)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}m\Sigma_{g\in G-\{0\}}d_{g}-m\Sigma_{j}a_{j}
is a positive integer multiple of
\operatorname{lcm}(a_{0},...,a_{r})
.

Further in this case, if
d=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}m\Sigma_{g\in G-\{0\}}d_{g}-m\Sigma_{j}a_{j}
, then
h^{0}(mK_{X})=p_{m}(X)=h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}}(d))-1
.

Proof.

f:X\to Y
is the
m-
canonical morphism if and only if the morphism
\varphi_{|mK_{X}|}
induced by the complete linear series
|mK_{X}|
for some
m\geq 1
factors as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{|mK_{X}|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{|\mathcal{O}_{Y}(d)|}}

Then we have the following two constraints which are in fact sufficient conditions as well :

(i)

f^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}(d))=mK_{X}

(ii)

h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}(d))=N+1=h^{0}(mK_{X})

We first analyze (i). This gives

\displaystyle f^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}(d))=f^{*}\left(mK_{Y}+m\sum_{H_{i},\Psi_{i}}\frac{(|H|-1)}{|H|}D_{H_{i},\Psi_{i}}\right)

Since
\operatorname{Pic}(Y)=\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{O}_{Y}(\operatorname{lcm}(a_{0},...,ar))
(see
[
10
, Theorem
3.2.4
]
), there are no degree zero line bundles on
Y
. Therefore, the above is equivalent to having

d=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}m\Sigma_{g\in G-\{0\}}d_{g}-m\Sigma_{j}a_{j}

Since we are assuming
\mathcal{O}_{Y}(d)
to be a line bundle, we have the second statement that
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}m\Sigma_{g\in G-\{0\}}d_{g}-m\Sigma_{j}a_{j}
should be a positive integer multiple of
\operatorname{lcm}(a_{0},...,a_{r})
.
Now condition (ii) implies that
h^{0}(f^{*}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}(d)))=h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}(d))
. By pushing forward, this is equivalent to
h^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}(d)\otimes L_{\chi}^{-1})=0

\forall\chi\in G^{*}-\{0\}
. By the fundamental relations, this implies that

d<\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{g\mid\chi(g)=1}d_{g}

and this implies the first condition.
∎

5.1.
Fourier Transform of functions on
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}

In this section, we prove some results on the Fourier transform of functions on
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
which are crucially used for the classification of flat pluricanonical covers of weighted projective threefolds.

Let
G=(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}
with additive notation.
Let
G^{*}
be the dual group of
G
.
Fix an isomorphism
G\to G^{*}
given by
g\mapsto\chi_{g}
, where
\chi_{g}(x)=(-1)^{g\cdot x}
, with
g\cdot x=\sum_{i=1}^{s}g_{i}x_{i}\pmod{2}
.
Via this isomorphism, we identify
G^{*}
with
G
, so that a character
\chi\in G^{*}
corresponds to a unique
g\in G
, and we write
\chi\cdot x=g\cdot x\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Lemma 5.1.1
.

Let
d:G\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with
d(0)=0
, and let
l:G^{*}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Define the Fourier transform of
d
as:

\widehat{d}(\chi)=\sum_{x\in G}d(x)\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}.

Suppose for all nontrivial
\chi\neq 0
:

(25)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in G\\
\chi\cdot x=1\end{subarray}}d(x)=2l(\chi).

Then for all
\chi\neq 0
:

\widehat{d}(0)-\widehat{d}(\chi)=4l(\chi),

where
\widehat{d}(0)=\sum_{x\in G}d(x)
, and
0
denotes the trivial character.

Proof.

Let
\chi\in G^{*}\setminus\{0\}
.
The set
\{x\in G:\chi\cdot x=1\}
has size
|G|/2=2^{s-1}
, since
\chi\cdot(\cdot)
is a nonzero linear functional.

Split the sum for
\widehat{d}(\chi)
:

\widehat{d}(\chi)=\sum_{\chi\cdot x=0}d(x)\cdot 1+\sum_{\chi\cdot x=1}d(x)\cdot(-1).

Let
S_{0}(\chi)=\sum_{\chi\cdot x=0}d(x)
,
S_{1}(\chi)=\sum_{\chi\cdot x=1}d(x)
.
Then:

\widehat{d}(\chi)=S_{0}(\chi)-S_{1}(\chi).

Now,
\widehat{d}(0)=\sum_{x\in G}d(x)\,(-1)^{0\cdot x}
.
But
0\cdot x=0
for all
x\in G
, so
(-1)^{0\cdot x}=1
.
Hence:

\widehat{d}(0)=\sum_{x\in G}d(x)=S_{0}(\chi)+S_{1}(\chi),

since
\{x:\chi\cdot x=0\}
and
\{x:\chi\cdot x=1\}
partition
G
.

From
Equation

25
,
S_{1}(\chi)=2l(\chi)
.

Thus:

\widehat{d}(0)-\widehat{d}(\chi)=[S_{0}+S_{1}]-[S_{0}-S_{1}]=2S_{1}(\chi)=4l(\chi).

This holds for all nontrivial
\chi
, completing the proof.

∎

Lemma 5.1.2
.

Let
G=(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}
with additive notation, and let
G^{*}
be its dual group, identified with
G
via
\chi_{g}(x)=(-1)^{g\cdot x}
.
Let
d:G\to\mathbb{C}
be any function, and define its Fourier transform
\widehat{d}:G^{*}\to\mathbb{C}
by

\widehat{d}(\chi)=\sum_{x\in G}d(x)\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}.

Then
d
can be recovered from
\widehat{d}
via the inverse formula:

d(x)=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d}(\chi)\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}.

Proof.

We substitute the definition of
\widehat{d}(\chi)
into the right-hand side:

\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d}(\chi)\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\left[\sum_{y\in G}d(y)\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot y}\right](-1)^{\chi\cdot x}.

Interchanging the order of summation:

=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{y\in G}d(y)\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}(-1)^{\chi\cdot(y+x)}.

Now, the inner sum
\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}(-1)^{\chi\cdot z}
(where
z=y+x
) is a standard character sum:

(1)

If
z=0
, then
(-1)^{\chi\cdot 0}=1
for all
\chi
, so the sum equals
|G^{*}|=|G|
.

(2)

If
z\neq 0
, then
\chi\mapsto(-1)^{\chi\cdot z}
is a nontrivial character of
G^{*}
, so the sum over
\chi\in G^{*}
is 0.

Thus:

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}(-1)^{\chi\cdot z}=\begin{cases}|G|&\text{if }z=0,\\
0&\text{if }z\neq 0.\end{cases}

Therefore, the only
y
contributing to the sum is
y=x
, giving:

\frac{1}{|G|}\cdot d(x)\cdot|G|=d(x).

This completes the proof.

∎

Lemma 5.1.3

(First moment constraint for the unnormalized Fourier transform)
.

Let
G=(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}
written additively, and identify the dual group
G^{*}
with
G

via
\chi_{g}(x)=(-1)^{g\cdot x}
, where
g\cdot x\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is the standard dot product.
For a function
f:G\to\mathbb{C}
, define its (unnormalized) Fourier transform by

\widehat{f}(\chi)\;=\;\sum_{x\in G}f(x)\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot x},\qquad\chi\in G^{*}.

Then

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{f}(\chi)\;=\;|G|\cdot f(0).

In particular, if
f(0)=0
then
\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{f}(\chi)=0
.

Proof.

We expand and interchange the order of summation:

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{f}(\chi)=\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\sum_{x\in G}f(x)\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}=\sum_{x\in G}f(x)\left(\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}\right).

We use the standard orthogonality of characters:

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}=\begin{cases}|G|,&x=0,\\
0,&x\neq 0,\end{cases}

since for
x\neq 0
the map
\chi\mapsto(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}
is a nontrivial character of

G^{*}
and its sum over the whole group is
0
. Hence only the term
x=0
contributes, and we
obtain

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{f}(\chi)=f(0)\cdot|G|.

This proves the claim.
∎

Lemma 5.1.4

(Parseval / inner product identity for the unnormalized Fourier transform)
.

Let
G=(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}
and identify
G^{*}\cong G
via

\chi_{g}(x)=(-1)^{g\cdot x},\qquad g\cdot x\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}.

For
f,g:G\to\mathbb{C}
, define the
unnormalized
Fourier transform

\widehat{f}(\chi)=\sum_{x\in G}f(x)\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot x},\qquad\chi\in G^{*}.

Then one has the Parseval identity

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{f}(\chi)\,\overline{\widehat{g}(\chi)}\;=\;|G|\sum_{x\in G}f(x)\,\overline{g(x)}.

Proof.

We expand and rearrange:

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{f}(\chi)\,\overline{\widehat{g}(\chi)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\left(\sum_{x\in G}f(x)(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}\right)\overline{\left(\sum_{y\in G}g(y)(-1)^{\chi\cdot y}\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\left(\sum_{x\in G}f(x)(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}\right)\left(\sum_{y\in G}\overline{g(y)}\,\overline{(-1)^{\chi\cdot y}}\right).

Since
(-1)^{\chi\cdot y}\in\{\pm 1\}\subset\mathbb{R}
, we have

\overline{(-1)^{\chi\cdot y}}=(-1)^{\chi\cdot y}
, hence

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{f}(\chi)\,\overline{\widehat{g}(\chi)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\sum_{x\in G}\sum_{y\in G}f(x)\,\overline{g(y)}\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot y}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in G}\sum_{y\in G}f(x)\,\overline{g(y)}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}(-1)^{\chi\cdot(x+y)}.

We now use the standard character-sum orthogonality:

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}(-1)^{\chi\cdot z}=\begin{cases}|G|&\text{if }z=0,\\
0&\text{if }z\neq 0.\end{cases}

Applying this with
z=x+y
(note
x+y=0
iff
x=y
), the inner sum equals
|G|

when
x=y
and vanishes otherwise. Therefore the double sum collapses to

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{f}(\chi)\,\overline{\widehat{g}(\chi)}=|G|\sum_{x\in G}f(x)\,\overline{g(x)},

as claimed.
∎

Lemma 5.1.5
.

With notation as above, for any
f:G\to\mathbb{C}
one has

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\bigl|\widehat{f}(\chi)\bigr|^{2}\;=\;|G|\sum_{x\in G}|f(x)|^{2}.

Proof.

Apply Parseval with
g=f
. Then
\overline{\widehat{f}(\chi)}
is the complex
conjugate of
\widehat{f}(\chi)
, so the left-hand side becomes

\sum_{\chi}\widehat{f}(\chi)\overline{\widehat{f}(\chi)}=\sum_{\chi}|\widehat{f}(\chi)|^{2}
.
Similarly, the right-hand side becomes
|G|\sum_{x}f(x)\overline{f(x)}=|G|\sum_{x}|f(x)|^{2}
. This is exactly the stated identity.
∎

Lemma 5.1.6

(Fourier transform of convolution and the cubic moment identity)
.

Let
G=(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}
written additively, and identify
G^{*}
with
G
via

\chi(x)=(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}
, where
\chi\cdot x\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
For a function
f:G\to\mathbb{C}
, define the (unnormalized) Fourier transform

\widehat{f}(\chi)=\sum_{x\in G}f(x)\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot x},\qquad\chi\in G^{*},

and define convolution by

(f*g)(z)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in G\\
x+y=z\end{subarray}}f(x)g(y),\qquad z\in G.

Then:

(1)

(Convolution becomes pointwise product.)
For all
\chi\in G^{*}
,

\widehat{f*g}(\chi)=\widehat{f}(\chi)\,\widehat{g}(\chi).

(2)

(Cubic moment at the origin.)
For any
d:G\to\mathbb{C}
,

(d*d*d)(0)=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d}(\chi)^{3}.

In particular, if
d=\mathbf{1}_{S}
is the indicator function of a subset
S\subseteq G
,
then

(d*d*d)(0)=\#\{(x,y,z)\in S^{3}:\ x+y+z=0\}\ \in\ \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

Proof.

(1)

Fix
\chi\in G^{*}
. By definition and expanding the convolution,

\widehat{f*g}(\chi)=\sum_{z\in G}(f*g)(z)\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot z}=\sum_{z\in G}\left(\sum_{x+y=z}f(x)g(y)\right)(-1)^{\chi\cdot z}.

Reindexing by
z=x+y
gives

\widehat{f*g}(\chi)=\sum_{x,y\in G}f(x)g(y)\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot(x+y)}.

Since
(-1)^{\chi\cdot(x+y)}=(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}(-1)^{\chi\cdot y}
, the sum factors:

\widehat{f*g}(\chi)=\left(\sum_{x\in G}f(x)(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}\right)\left(\sum_{y\in G}g(y)(-1)^{\chi\cdot y}\right)=\widehat{f}(\chi)\,\widehat{g}(\chi),

as required.

(2)

Apply (1) twice to obtain
\widehat{d*d*d}(\chi)=\widehat{d}(\chi)^{3}
for all
\chi
.
Now use the inverse Fourier transform (with this unnormalized convention):

h(0)=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{h}(\chi)\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot 0}=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{h}(\chi),

and take
h=d*d*d
. This yields

(d*d*d)(0)=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d*d*d}(\chi)=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d}(\chi)^{3}.

Finally, if
d=\mathbf{1}_{S}
, then by the definition of convolution,

(d*d*d)(0)=\sum_{x+y+z=0}d(x)d(y)d(z)=\#\{(x,y,z)\in S^{3}:\ x+y+z=0\},

which is a nonnegative integer.

∎

5.2.
Lemmas related to the pluricanonical map

Here, we prove some combinatorial lemmas required for the classification of pluricanonical covers of weighted projective threefolds. Fix four positive integers
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
satisfying

\gcd(a_{i},a_{j},a_{k})=1
for every triple of distinct indices
i,j,k
. Define:

W:=a_{0}+a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3},\quad L:=\operatorname{lcm}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}).

For
g
and
\chi
in
(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}\setminus 0
define the dot product
\chi\cdot g
as the usual dot product modulo
2
. For each
g\in(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}\setminus 0
, we associate an integer
d_{g}\geq 0
, and we have

\displaystyle D:=\sum_{g\in(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}\setminus 0}d_{g}

\displaystyle l_{\chi}\;=\;\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\in(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}\setminus 0\\
\chi\cdot g=1\end{subarray}}d_{g}

\displaystyle M:=\frac{m}{2}D-mW

Next, we encode in a definition the conditions given by
Proposition

5.0.1
.

Definition 5.2.1
.

The list of numbers

(d_{g},\,m,\,s)\in\mathbb{Z}^{|2^{s}-1|}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
is called a
admissible pluricanonical solution
for the weights
(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
if it satisfies:

(1)

For all
\chi\in(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}\setminus 0
,
l_{\chi}
is an integer.

(2)

For all
\chi\in(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}\setminus 0
, it holds that

M
is a positive integer, divisible by
L
, and strictly less than
l_{\chi}
.

The solution is called
flat
if
l_{\chi}
is divisible by
L
for
all
\chi
.

Lemma 5.2.2
.

Let the notations be as above in this subsection, we suppose that
there is an admissible pluricanonical solution with respect to some weights.
Then, the following holds:

1.

If
L\geq 2
, then
3L\geq 2L+2\geq W.

2.

Let’s denote
M=kL
, if there exists an admissible flat solution for given weights, then the following inequality holds

\displaystyle W\geq\left((k+1)(2-2^{1-s})-\frac{k}{m}\right)L

\displaystyle l_{\chi}\geq(k+1)L,\;\;\forall\chi

3.

Assume
L\geq 2
. Then any admissible flat solution with
s\geq 2
and
m\geq 3
satisfies
3\geq L
and
M=L
.

Proof.

We start with
statement
1
.
Let
b_{i}:=L/a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Then

\frac{W}{L}=\sum_{i=0}^{3}\frac{a_{i}}{L}=\sum_{i=0}^{3}\frac{1}{b_{i}}.

If
b_{i}\geq 2
for four or three indices then the inequality
\sum_{i}\frac{1}{b_{i}}\leq 2+\frac{2}{L}
follows easily. Suppose that there are exactly two
i
such that
b_{i}=1
. Without loss of generality
b_{0}=b_{1}=1
, i.e.
a_{0}=a_{1}=L
.
From
\gcd(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2})=1
we get
\gcd(L,a_{2})=1
, and since
a_{2}\mid L
, we must have
a_{2}=1
. Similarly
a_{3}=1
. Thus
b_{2}=b_{3}=L
, and

\sum_{i=0}^{3}\frac{1}{b_{i}}=1+1+\frac{1}{L}+\frac{1}{L}=2+\frac{2}{L}\leq 3\quad\text{because }L\geq 2.

If
b_{0}=b_{1}=b_{2}=1
, then
a_{0}=a_{1}=a_{2}=L
, so
\gcd(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2})=L>1
, contradicting the hypothesis on the weights. Then, this case cannot occur
when
L>1
. The same argument applies when
b_{i}=1
for all
i
.

We continue with
statement 2.

By the definition of
l_{\chi}
and the condition that
l_{\chi}>M
with
M=kL
for some positive integer
k
, we obtain that

\displaystyle 2^{\,s-2}\,D\;=\;\sum_{\chi}l_{\chi},

\displaystyle l_{\chi}>kL

Moreover, if the solution is flat, we have

(26)

\displaystyle 2^{\,s-2}\,D\;=\;\sum_{\chi}l_{\chi},

\displaystyle l_{\chi}\geq(k+1)L

From these two last expressions, we obtain

(27)

\displaystyle 2^{s-2}D\geq(2^{s}-1)(k+1)L\Longrightarrow\;\;D\geq(4-2^{2-s})(k+1)L

Since, we have

(28)

\displaystyle\frac{m}{2}D-mW=kL\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;W=\frac{1}{2}D-\frac{k}{m}L

, we obtain the lower bound

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}D-\frac{k}{m}L

(29)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(k+1)}{2}\left(4-2^{2-s}\right)L-\frac{k}{m}L

\displaystyle\geq\left((k+1)(2-2^{1-s})-\frac{k}{m}\right)L

if the solution is flat.
We finish with
statement 3:

By statements 1 and 2, it holds that

\displaystyle 2+\frac{2}{L}\geq\frac{W}{L}\geq(k+1)(2-2^{1-s})-\frac{k}{m}

For
s\geq 2
, we have that
(2-2^{1-s})\geq\frac{3}{2}
, therefore

\displaystyle(k+1)(2-2^{1-s})\geq\frac{3}{2}(k+1)\Longrightarrow\;\;(k+1)(2-2^{1-s})-\frac{k}{m}\geq\frac{3}{2}(k+1)-\frac{k}{m}

the condition
m\geq 3
implies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\leq\frac{1}{3}\Longrightarrow\;\;-\frac{k}{m}\geq-\frac{k}{3}\Longrightarrow\;\;\frac{3}{2}(k+1)-\frac{k}{m}\geq\frac{3}{2}(k+1)-\frac{k}{3}

Then, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{W}{L}\geq(k+1)(2-2^{1-s})-\frac{k}{m}\geq\frac{7k}{6}+\frac{3}{2}

Since
\frac{W}{L}\leq 3
, we have that
k=1
. We further have that

\displaystyle 2+\frac{2}{L}\geq\frac{W}{L}\geq\frac{7k}{6}+\frac{3}{2}\geq\frac{8}{3}

which implies
L\leq 3
.

∎

Lemma 5.2.3
.

Let the notations be as above in this subsection, we suppose
L\geq 2
.
Let
s\geq 2
, and
m\geq 1
, if
s
and
m
satisfy one of the following inequalities.

\displaystyle s\geq 2,\;m\geq 4

\displaystyle s\geq 3,\;m\geq 3

\displaystyle s\geq 4,\;m\geq 2

\displaystyle s\geq 6,m\geq 1

Then, there are no flat admissible solutions.

Proof.

First, we consider the case
s\geq 3
and
m\geq 3
.
and suppose the solution is flat. By statement 3 in Lemma
5.2.2
, we have that
k=1
.
By (
27
),
s\geq 3
imply

\displaystyle\frac{D}{L}\geq 7

\displaystyle 3\geq\frac{W}{L}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{D}{L}-\frac{1}{m}\geq\frac{7}{2}-\frac{1}{m}

We expand the last inequality to obtain
m\leq 2
which is a contradiction with
m\geq 3
.

Next, we consider the case
s\geq 2
and
m\geq 4
. By Lemma
5.2.2
(3) we have

k=1
and
L\leq 3.
From (
27
) with
s=2
we obtain

\frac{D}{L}\;\geq\;\bigl(4-2^{\,2-2}\bigr)(k+1)\;=\;3\cdot 2\;=\;6.

Using
M=\frac{m}{2}D-mW=kL
with
k=1
we have

(30)

\displaystyle\frac{W}{L}\;=\;\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{D}{L}-\frac{1}{m}.

Lemma
5.2.2
.(1) and the fact that
L\geq 2
and
m\geq 4
imply

\frac{W}{L}\leq 2+\frac{2}{L}\leq 3,\quad\quad\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{D}{L}-\frac{1}{m}\;\leq\;2+\frac{2}{L}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\frac{D}{L}\;\leq\;4+\frac{4}{L}+\frac{2}{m}\;\leq\;\frac{13}{2}.

For
s=2
we have
2^{\,s-2}D=\sum_{\chi}l_{\chi}=D
, and by flatness
L\mid l_{\chi}
for all
\chi
, so
L\mid D
; therefore
\frac{D}{L}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then, we obtain that

6\leq\frac{D}{L}\leq\frac{13}{2}.\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\frac{D}{L}=6,\qquad D=6L.

We use
l_{\chi}\geq 2L
and

\sum_{\chi}l_{\chi}=D=6L,

it follows that

l_{10}=l_{01}=l_{11}=2L.

Using the fundamental relations for
G=(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{2}
,

2l_{10}=d_{10}+d_{11},\qquad 2l_{01}=d_{01}+d_{11},\qquad 2l_{11}=d_{10}+d_{01},

we solve and get
d_{10}=d_{01}=d_{11}=2L.
Finally, from
Equation (
30
) and
D=6L
we obtain

W=\Bigl(3-\frac{1}{m}\Bigr)L.

If
L=3
, then
\frac{W}{L}=3-\frac{1}{m}\geq\frac{11}{4}>2+\frac{2}{3}
which is a contradiction. If
L=2
, then

W=6-\frac{2}{m}\notin\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{for every }m\geq 4,

which is impossible since
W=\sum_{i}a_{i}
is an integer. Therefore, no flat admissible solution exists when
s=2
and
m\geq 4
.

Next, we consider the case
s\geq 4
and
m\geq 2
. By (
29
) we have

\frac{W}{L}\;\geq\;(k+1)\!\left(2-2^{1-s}\right)-\frac{k}{m}.

For
s\geq 4
we get
2-2^{1-s}\geq 2-2^{-3}=\tfrac{15}{8}
, and for
m\geq 2
we have
\tfrac{k}{m}\leq\tfrac{k}{2}
. Hence

\frac{W}{L}\;\geq\;(k+1)\,\frac{15}{8}-\frac{k}{2}\;=\;\frac{15}{8}+\frac{11}{8}\,k\;\geq\;\frac{15}{8}+\frac{11}{8}\;=\;\frac{13}{4}\;>\;3.

On the other hand, by Lemma
5.2.2
, we have
\frac{W}{L}\leq 2+\frac{2}{L}\leq 3
, a contradiction.

Finally, we work on the case
s\geq 6
and
m=1
.
For
s\geq 6
we get
2-2^{1-s}\geq 2-2^{-5}=\tfrac{63}{32}
and since
m=1

Equation (
29
) implies

\frac{W}{L}\;\geq\;(k+1)\!\left(2-2^{1-s}\right)-k\geq\;\frac{63}{32}(k+1)-k=\frac{31}{32}\,k+\frac{63}{32}.

This last inequality implies
k=1
. Otherwise
W/L>3
, which is a contradiction.
We arrive to

\displaystyle 2+\frac{2}{L}\geq\frac{W}{L}\geq\frac{47}{16}

which implies
L=2
. Using
M=\frac{m}{2}D-mW
with
m=1
and
M=kL=2
, we obtain

\frac{1}{2}D-W=2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad W=\frac{1}{2}D-2,\quad W\leq 2L+2=6

Previous discussion implies

\frac{47}{8}\leq W\leq 6\quad\Longrightarrow\quad W=6,\quad D=16

Now note that
l_{\chi}\geq 4
and
2\mid l_{\chi}
for all
\chi\neq 0
. Further
\Sigma_{\chi\in G^{*}-\{0\}}l_{\chi}=2^{s-2}D=2^{s+2}
. On the other hand there are
2^{s}-1
non-zero characters. This forces the following two tuples of
\{l_{\chi}\}

(1)

There exists
\chi_{0}\neq 0
such that
l(\chi_{0})=8
, and
l(\chi)=4
for all
\chi\neq\{0,\chi_{0}\}
or

(2)

There exist distinct
\chi_{0},\chi_{1}\neq 0
such that
l(\chi_{0})=l(\chi_{1})=6
, and
l(\chi)=4
for all
\chi\neq\{0,\chi_{0},\chi_{1}\}
.

However, such solution does not exist by
Lemma

5.2.4

∎

Lemma 5.2.4
.

Let
G=(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}
with
s\geq 6
, and let
G^{*}
be its dual group, identified with
G
via
\chi_{g}(x)=(-1)^{g\cdot x}
.
Let
l:G^{*}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with
l(0)=0
, and suppose either:

(1)

There exists
\chi_{0}\neq 0
such that
l(\chi_{0})=8
, and
l(\chi)=4
for all
\chi\neq\{0,\chi_{0}\}
or

(2)

There exist distinct
\chi_{0},\chi_{1}\neq 0
such that
l(\chi_{0})=l(\chi_{1})=6
, and
l(\chi)=4
for all
\chi\neq\{0,\chi_{0},\chi_{1}\}
.

Then there is no function
d:G\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with
d(0)=0
satisfying

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\in G\\
\chi\cdot g=1\end{subarray}}d(g)=2l(\chi)\quad\text{for all }\chi\neq 0.

Proof.

Assume such a
d
exists. By
Lemma

5.1.1
,

\widehat{d}(0)-\widehat{d}(\chi)=4l(\chi)\quad\forall\chi\neq 0,

where
\widehat{d}(\chi)=\sum_{x\in G}d(x)(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}
, and
\widehat{d}(0)=\sum_{x}d(x)
.

Step 1: Determine
\widehat{d}(0)
from
d(0)=0

Apply the inverse Fourier transform at
x=0
:

d(0)=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d}(\chi)(-1)^{\chi\cdot 0}=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d}(\chi).

Since
d(0)=0
, we have

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d}(\chi)=0.

Split the sum:
\widehat{d}(0)+\sum_{\chi\neq 0}\widehat{d}(\chi)=0
.

From
\widehat{d}(0)-\widehat{d}(\chi)=4l(\chi)
, we get
\widehat{d}(\chi)=\widehat{d}(0)-4l(\chi)
.

So:

\widehat{d}(0)+\sum_{\chi\neq 0}\left[\widehat{d}(0)-4l(\chi)\right]=0.

There are
|G|-1=2^{s}-1
nonzero
\chi
. Thus:

\widehat{d}(0)+(2^{s}-1)\widehat{d}(0)-4\sum_{\chi\neq 0}l(\chi)=0.

That is:

2^{s}\widehat{d}(0)-4\sum_{\chi\neq 0}l(\chi)=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\widehat{d}(0)=\frac{4}{2^{s}}\sum_{\chi\neq 0}l(\chi).

Step 2: Compute
\widehat{d}(0)
for each case

Case 1:

One
\chi_{0}
with
l=8
, and
2^{s}-2
others with
l=4
.
So:

\sum_{\chi\neq 0}l(\chi)=8+(2^{s}-2)\cdot 4=8+4\cdot 2^{s}-8=4\cdot 2^{s}.

Thus
\widehat{d}(0)=\frac{4}{2^{s}}\cdot 4\cdot 2^{s}=16
.

Case 2:

Two
\chi
with
l=6
, and
2^{s}-3
with
l=4
.
So:

\sum_{\chi\neq 0}l(\chi)=2\cdot 6+(2^{s}-3)\cdot 4=12+4\cdot 2^{s}-12=4\cdot 2^{s}.

Again
\widehat{d}(0)=16
. So in both cases,
\widehat{d}(0)=16
.

Step 3: Determine the values of
\widehat{d}(\chi)

Case 1:

\widehat{d}(\chi_{0})=16-4\cdot 8=-16
,

\widehat{d}(\chi)=16-4\cdot 4=0
for
\chi\neq 0,\chi_{0}
.

Case 2:

\widehat{d}(\chi_{0})=\widehat{d}(\chi_{1})=16-4\cdot 6=-8
,

\widehat{d}(\chi)=0
for
\chi\neq 0,\chi_{0},\chi_{1}
.

Step 4: Apply inverse Fourier transform

By
Lemma

5.1.2
,:

d(x)=\frac{1}{2^{s}}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d}(\chi)(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}.

Case 1:

Only
\chi=0
and
\chi=\chi_{0}
contribute:

d(x)=\frac{1}{2^{s}}\left[16+(-16)(-1)^{\chi_{0}\cdot x}\right]=\frac{16}{2^{s}}\left[1-(-1)^{\chi_{0}\cdot x}\right].

If
\chi_{0}\cdot x=0
,
d(x)=0
.
If
\chi_{0}\cdot x=1
,
d(x)=\frac{16}{2^{s}}\cdot 2=\frac{32}{2^{s}}
.

For
s\geq 6
,
2^{s}\geq 64
, so
\frac{32}{2^{s}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
, and is an integer only if
2^{s}\mid 32
, i.e.,
s\leq 5
.
Thus for
s\geq 6
,
d(x)
is not an integer for some
x
.

Case 2:

Contributions from
\chi=0,\chi_{0},\chi_{1}
:

d(x)=\frac{1}{2^{s}}\left[16+(-8)(-1)^{\chi_{0}\cdot x}+(-8)(-1)^{\chi_{1}\cdot x}\right].

Let
a=(-1)^{\chi_{0}\cdot x}
,
b=(-1)^{\chi_{1}\cdot x}
, each
\pm 1
.

(1)

a=1,b=1
:
d(x)=\frac{1}{2^{s}}[16-16]=0
.

(2)

a=1,b=-1
:
d(x)=\frac{1}{2^{s}}[16-0]=\frac{16}{2^{s}}
.

(3)

a=-1,b=1
:
d(x)=\frac{16}{2^{s}}
.

(4)

a=-1,b=-1
:
d(x)=\frac{1}{2^{s}}[16+16]=\frac{32}{2^{s}}
.

For
s\geq 6
,
\frac{16}{2^{s}}
and
\frac{32}{2^{s}}
are not integers.

Thus in both cases,
d(x)
is not integer-valued for
s\geq 6
, contradicting the assumption.
Therefore, no such
d
exists.

∎

5.3.
Proof Theorem
1.3.1

By Lemma
5.2.3
, we only need to consider the list of
s
and
m
within the statement. In principle, there is an infinite number of possible weights and degrees to consider. Our first step is to bound the number of possible weights and degrees to a final list of tuples

(L,W,D,k)
that must be satisfied by any solution. We then find all possible cases using computational techniques.

We start by bounding the number of possible cases. We recall the identities and bounds already established:

M=\frac{m}{2}D-mW=kL,\qquad L\geq 2,\qquad W\leq 2L+2,

and, writing
\beta(s):=2-2^{1-s}
,

(31)

\frac{W}{L}\ \geq\ (k+1)\,\beta(s)\ -\ \frac{k}{m},\qquad D\ \geq\ \bigl(4-2^{2-s}\bigr)(k+1)\,L,

which are (
29
), (
27
) and Statement 1 of Lemma
5.2.2
.
We also use the exact relation

(32)

D\ =\ 2W+\frac{2k}{m}\,L,

and the divisibility
m\mid 2kL
(since
m(D-2W)=2kL
).
For
s=2
, it also holds that
L\mid D
because
D=\sum_{\chi}l_{\chi}
and
L\mid l_{\chi}
for all
\chi
.

Proof of Case
(s,m)=(2,3)

Case . By Lemma
5.2.2
.3 (with
s\geq 2
,
m\geq 3
) we have
k=1
and
L\leq 3
.
Since
3\mid 2L
, necessarily
L=3
. From (
31
):

\frac{W}{L}\ \geq\ 2\beta(2)-\frac{1}{3}\ =\ 2\cdot\frac{3}{2}-\frac{1}{3}\ =\ \frac{8}{3}.

Together with
W/L\leq 2+\frac{2}{L}=2+\frac{2}{3}
we obtain
W=8
. Then (
32
) gives

D=2W+\tfrac{2}{3}L=16+2=18
. For
s=2
,
L\mid D
holds.
This implies each
l_{\chi}=6
because
\sum_{\chi}l_{\chi}=D=18
and each
l_{\chi}\geq 2L=6
and multiple of
L
. Therefore,
d_{10}=d_{01}=d_{11}=6
.

Proof of Case
(s,m)=(2,2)

Case
(s,m)=(2,2)
. From (
31
) and
W/L\leq 3
, we obtain
k=1
.
This implies

\frac{W}{L}\ \in\Bigl[\ \tfrac{5}{2}\ ,\ 2+\tfrac{2}{L}\ \Bigr],\qquad L\geq 2.

Our possible values
L\in\{2,3,4\}
. A brief check gives

(L,W)\in\{(2,5),(2,6),(4,10),(3,8)\},

From (
32
),
D=2W+L
implies that for
(L,W)=(3,8)
,
D=19
. But since
3\nmid 19
, the pair
(3,8)
is excluded. Hence again from (
32
),

D=2W+L\in\{12,14,24\}
;

So we consider the pairs
(L,W,D)=(2,5,12),(2,6,14),(4,10,24)
. Note that since
s=2
, by
Equation

26
,
\Sigma_{\chi\in G^{*}}l_{\chi}=D
. Moreover since
l_{\chi}\geq 2L=4
and even, we have that in each of these cases,
\{l_{\chi}\}_{\chi\in G^{*}}=(4,4,4),(4,4,6),(8,8,8)
. Now calculating the branch divisors
\{d_{g}\}_{g\in G}
gives the result.

Proof of Case
(s,m)=(2,1)

Case
(s,m)=(2,1)
:

From (
31
)

\frac{W}{L}\ \geq\ (k+1)\frac{3}{2}-k\ =\ \frac{k+3}{2}.

Since
W/L\leq 3
, necessarily
k\in\{1,2,3\}
. We enumerate per
k
, enforcing
L\geq 2
,
W\leq 2L+2
, and (
32
). First, we suppose
k=1
. In this case, we note that
2L+2\geq W\geq 2L
. This means
W\in\{2L,2L+1,2L+2\}
. If
W=2L+1
, we have that
D=2W+\frac{2k}{m}L=6L+2
. Since
s=2
, we have that
L\mid D
and hence
L=2
. Hence we get
(L,W,D)=(2,5,14)
. Similar analysis with
W=2L+2
yields
L=2
or
L=4
.
L=2
yields
(L,W,D)=(2,6,16)
while
L=4
yields
(4,10,28)
. Now consider
W=2L
. We do not directly get an upper bound for
L
as above, but similar analysis yields
(L,W,D)=(L,2L,6L)
. But now it is easy to see that the only solutions for a set of positive integers
(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
such that
\Sigma_{i}a_{i}=2L
and
\operatorname{lcm}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=L
are
(1,1,1,3),(1,1,2,4),(1,2,3,6)
and in particular
L=3,4,6
. This yields the triples
(L,W,D)=(3,6,18),(4,8,24),(6,12,36)
. Hence the triples we obtain are

(L,W,D)\in\{(2,5,14),\ (2,6,16),\ (3,6,18),\ (4,8,24),\ (4,10,28),\ (6,12,36)\}.

Consider
(L,W,D)=(2,5,14)
. This yields
\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,2)
. Since
\Sigma_{\chi\in G^{*}-\{0\}}l_{\chi}=D=14
,
2\mid l_{\chi}
and
l_{\chi}\geq 2L=4
, we have
\{l_{\chi}\}=(4,4,6)
which yields
d_{g}=(2,6,6)
. Consider
(L,W,D)=(2,6,16)
. This yields
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2,2)
and
d_{g}=(8,8,0)
or
(4,4,8)
. Consider
(L,W,D)=(4,10,28)
. This gives
\mathbb{P}(1,1,4,4)
and
d_{g}=(4,12,12)
. Consider
(L,W,D)=(3,6,18)
. This gives
\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,3)
and
d_{g}=(6,6,6)
. Consider
(L,W,D)=(4,8,24)
. This gives
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2,4)
and
d_{g}=(8,8,8)
. Consider
(L,W,D)=(6,12,36)
. This gives
\mathbb{P}(1,2,3,6)
and
d_{g}=(12,12,12)
.

Next, we suppose
k=2
. We obtain

(L,W,D)\in\{(2,5,18),\ (2,6,20),\ (4,10,36),\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(3,9,30),\ (4,12,40)}\},

Note that there is no choice of
\mathbb{P}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
with
W=9
and
L=3
or
W=12
and
L=4
. Now
(2,5,18)
yields
d=(6,6,6)
,
(2,6,20)
yields
d=(4,8,8)
and
(4,10,36)
yields
d=(12,12,12)

Finally, we suppose
k=3
. We obtain uniquely

(L,W,D)=(2,6,24),

with
d=(8,8,8)
forced.

Proof of Case
(s,m)=(3,2)

Case
(s,m)=(3,2)
:

From (
31
) we have

\frac{W}{L}\ \geq\ (k+1)\frac{7}{4}-\frac{k}{2}\ =\ \frac{5k+7}{4}.

The bound
W/L\leq 3
forces
k=1
, and then
W/L\geq 3
. Since
2+\frac{2}{L}\leq 3
, this yields
L=2
,
W=6
.
By (
32
),
D=14
. Setting
N_{\chi}=2l_{\chi}
, we have
\sum_{\chi}N_{\chi}=2^{2}D=56
and
N_{\chi}\in 4\mathbb{Z}
,
N_{\chi}\geq 8
;
hence each
N_{\chi}=8
and
l_{\chi}=4
.

Proof of Case
(s,m)=(3,1)

Case
(s,m)=(3,1)
.

From (
31
) we have
\frac{W}{L}\geq(k+1)\frac{7}{4}-k=\frac{3k+7}{4}
; thus
k=1
.
Hence

\frac{W}{L}\in\Bigl[\tfrac{5}{2},\ 2+\tfrac{2}{L}\Bigr],\qquad L\geq 2,

which yields
(L,W,D)\in\{(2,5,14),(2,6,16),(3,8,22),(3,9,24),(4,10,28),(4,11,30),(4,12,32)\}
. But now there are no solutions of well-formed
\mathbb{P}(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})
with
(W=9,L=3)
,
(W=11,L=4)
and
(W=12,L=4)
. The triple
(2,5,14)
yields
\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,2)
and
\{l_{\chi}\}=(4,4,4,4,4,4,4)
, the triple
(2,6,16)
yields
\mathbb{P}(1,1,2,2)
and
\{l_{\chi}\}=(8,4,4,4,4,4,4)
or
(4,4,6,6,4,4,4)
. The triple
(3,8,22)
yields
\Sigma_{\chi\in G^{*}-\{0\}}l_{\chi}=44
,
l_{\chi}\geq 6
and
3\mid l_{\chi}
for all
\chi
. This does not yield any solution for
\{l_{\chi}\}
. Finally the triple
(4,10,28)
yields
\mathbb{P}(1,1,4,4)
and
\{l_{\chi}\}=(8,8,8,8,8,8,8)
. Now the values of
d_{g}
can be computed from
\{l_{\chi}\}
using the fundamental equations and the value of
D
.

Proof of Case

,

,

and

. We show this for

),
\frac{W}{L}\geq(k+1)\frac{15}{8}-k=\frac{7k+15}{8}

, and since

, we get

and, by (

),

.

Assume flatness and the standing notation of this subsection. Then any flat admissible solution with
s=4
and

k=1,\qquad L=2,\qquad W=6,\qquad D=16,

Now note that
l_{\chi}\geq(k+1)L=4
and

. Further
\Sigma_{\chi\in G^{*}-\{0\}}l_{\chi}=2^{s-2}D=64
. Hence there are two cases

(1)

: There exists a
\chi_{0}\in G^{*}-\{0\}
such that

and

for

and

.

(2)

and

for

,

.

For

, we get

(1)

if

(there are

(there are

For

.

5.4.
Pluricanonical covers of
\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,1)

We continue with the notation of this section to classify the pluricanonical
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{s}
covers of
\mathbb{P}(1,1,1,1)
. Hence
W=4
and
L=1
. Note that in this case
M=k=m(\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}D-4)
, where
D=\sum_{g}d_{g}
and hence
D=8+\frac{2k}{m}
. Further recall that
s=1
has already been classified.

Lemma 5.4.1
.

(1)

If
m\geq 3
and
s\geq 2
, then
k=2
,
m=4
and
\sum_{g}d_{g}=9

(2)

If
m\geq 3
and
s\geq 3
, then there are no admissible solutions.

(3)

If
m\geq 5
and
s\geq 2
, then there are no admissible solutions.

Proof.

Proof of
(1)
: By
Lemma

5.2.2
,
(2)
, we have that

\displaystyle W\geq\left((k+1)(2-2^{1-s})-\frac{k}{m}\right)L

\displaystyle l_{\chi}\geq(k+1)L,\;\;\forall\chi

Since
W=4
and
L=1
, we have

\left((k+1)(2-2^{1-s})-\frac{k}{m}\right)\leq 4

Since
s\geq 2
and
m\geq 3
, we have that the LHS
\geq\displaystyle\frac{7k}{6}+\frac{3}{2}
. Hence
k\leq 2
.

Suppose that
k=1
. Then
M=kL=1
. Recall that
M=m(\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{g}d_{g}-4)
. Hence we have

m(\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{g}d_{g}-4)=1

This implies that
\sum_{g}d_{g}=8+\displaystyle\frac{2}{m}
. This implies that
m=1
or
m=2
, contradicting
m\geq 3
.

If
k=2
, then
M=2
and
\sum_{g}d_{g}=8+\displaystyle\frac{4}{m}
. Then
m\mid 4
and
m\geq 3\implies m=4
. Therefore
\sum_{g}d_{g}=9
.

Proof of
(2)
.
Now suppose that
m\geq 3
and
s\geq 3
. From part
(1)
,
k=1
or
k=2
. Suppose that
k=2
. Then we have from
Lemma

5.2.2
,
(2)
,

\left((3)(2-2^{1-s})-\frac{2}{m}\right)\leq 4

which, since
s\geq 3
, gives
m\leq\displaystyle\frac{8}{5}
contradicting
m\geq 3
.

Now assume
k=1
. This implies
M=1
and hence
\sum_{g}d_{g}=8+\displaystyle\frac{2}{m}
. This implies
m\mid 2
and contradicts
m\geq 3
.

Proof of
(3)
.
If
m\geq 5
and
s\geq 2
, once again by part
(1)
, we have
k=1
or
k=2
.
k=2\implies M=2\implies\sum_{g}d_{g}=8+\displaystyle\frac{4}{m}\implies m\mid 4
, which is a contradiction. Similarly
k=1\implies m\mid 2
, which leads to a contradiction.

∎

Lemma 5.4.2
.

(1)

If
m=2
and
k\geq 2
, then
s\leq 2

(2)

If
m=2
and
k\geq 3
, then
s\leq 1

(3)

If
m=1
and
k\geq 3
, then
s\leq 3

(4)

If
m=1
and
k\geq 4
, then
s\leq 2

(5)

If
m=1
and
k\geq 6
, then
s\leq 1

Proof.

Proof of
(1)
and
(2)
.
Recall that we have

\left((k+1)(2-2^{1-s})-\frac{k}{m}\right)\leq 4

If
m=2
, the above gives

2^{s}\leq\displaystyle\frac{4k+4}{3k-4}

Hence
k\geq 2\implies s\leq 2
and if
k\geq 3\implies s\leq 1
.

Proof of
(3)
,
(4)
and
(5)
.

Using

\left((k+1)(2-2^{1-s})-\frac{k}{m}\right)\leq 4

if
m=1
, we get

2^{s}\leq\displaystyle\frac{2k+2}{k-2}

Now the conclusion follows by plugging in the corresponding values of
k
.

∎

.

The values of

,

,

and

for which there are possible admissible solutions are as follows:

,

,

(2)

,

(3)

,

,

(4)

m=1
,
s=3
,
k=3\iff D=14

(5)

m=1
,
s=2
,
k=4\iff D=16

(6)

m=1
,
s=2
,
k=5\iff D=18

(7)

m=2
,

,
k=1\iff D=9

(8)

m=2
,
s=2
,
k=2\iff D=10

(9)

m=4
,
s=2
,

Proof.

Follows by combining

5.4.1
and

.
∎

Theorem 5.4.4
.

Upto
\operatorname{GL}(s,\mathbb{F}_{2})
action

(1)

For

,

and

and

,
(d_{10},d_{01},d_{11})=(3,3,3)

(2)

and

and

,

(3)

For

,

and

and

,
(d_{10},d_{01},d_{11})=(6,6,6)

(4)

For

,

,

(5)

Proof.

The proof follows from
Corollary

5.4.3
, and the facts that
\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}l_{\chi}=2^{s-2}D
,
l_{\chi}\geq(k+1)
for
\chi\neq 0
, and
2l_{\chi}=\sum_{\{g\in G|\chi\cdot g=1\}}d_{g}
.
∎

The remaining cases are
(1)
,
(2)
and
(7)
of
Corollary

5.4.3
. However, since
m=1,k=1
corresponds to canonical covers of
\mathbb{P}^{3}
with
p_{g}=4
, this is done in
[
11
]
. So we analyze cases
(2)
and
(7)
of
Corollary

5.4.3
.

We first state a Proposition to bound the value of
s
, given an upper bound to
D
.

Proposition 5.4.5
.

Let
G=(\mathbb{Z}_{2})^{s}
. Let
d:G\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
satisfy

d(0)=0,\qquad\sum_{g\in G}d(g)=C,

and suppose there exists an integer
p\geq 1
such that for every nonzero
character
\chi\in G^{*}
,

\sum_{\chi(g)=1}d(g)\geq p.

Let

S:=\{g\in G:d(g)>0\}

Then

(1)

s\leq|S|

(2)

Suppose
s\geq t+1
and let
g_{1},\dots,g_{t}\in S
be distinct. Then

\sum_{i=1}^{t}d(g_{i})\leq C-p.

(3)

In particular,

s\leq C-p+1.

Proof.

Let

S:=\{g\in G:d(g)>0\}

denote the support of
d
. Then
|S|\leq C
.

Proof of
(1)
.
Suppose
\langle S\rangle
is a proper subspace of
G
. Then there exists a nonzero character
\chi\in G^{*}
such that
\chi(g)=0
for all
g\in S
. For
this
\chi
,

\sum_{\chi(g)=1}d(g)=0,

contradicting
(\star)
. Hence
\langle S\rangle=G
, and therefore

s=\dim G\leq|S|\leq C.

Proof of
(2)
.
Let
t
be a positive integer. If
s\geq t+1
, then for any choice of
t
distinct elements
g_{1},\dots,g_{t}\in G
, there exists a nonzero character

\chi\in G^{*}
such that

\chi(g_{1})=\cdots=\chi(g_{t})=0.

Assume
s\geq t+1
and let
g_{1},\dots,g_{t}\in S
be distinct. Choose
\chi
as in
Step
2
. Then

\sum_{\chi(g)=1}d(g)\leq C-\sum_{i=1}^{t}d(g_{i}).

By
(\star)
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{t}d(g_{i})\leq C-p.

Thus, if
s\geq t+1
, every
t
-subset of the support has total weight at most

C-p
.

Proof of
(3)
.
Set

t:=C-p+1.

If
s\geq t+1=C-p+2
, then by (1) every subset of
S
of size
t
has total
weight at most
C-p
which is a contradiction since
d(g)>0
for
g\in S
.
∎

Now we use the above proposition as a base case and sharpen the upper bound to
s
for each of cases
(2)
and
(7)
in
Corollary

5.4.3
. From now on, define
m_{i}:=\#\{g\neq 0:d(g)=i\}
and
n_{i}:=\#\{\chi\neq 0:\ell(\chi)=i\}
. Recall that
d(0)=\ell(0)=0

Then

Lemma 5.4.6
.

Let
S:=\{g\in G:d(g)>0\}
. In
Corollary

5.4.3
,

(1)

in item
(2)
,
s\leq 7

(2)

in item
(2)
, if
s=7
,
|S|=12
and
d_{g}=1
for all
g\in S
.

(3)

in item
(2)
, if

, then

. If
m_{i}=|g\in S,d_{g}=i|
, then the possible tuples are
(m_{1})=12

.

(4)

in item
(2)
, if
s=5
, then for
g\in S
,
d_{g}\in\{1,2,3\}
. If
m_{i}=|g\in S,d_{g}=i|
, then the possible tuples
(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})
are
(m_{1},m_{3})=(9,1)
,
(m_{1},m_{2})=(8,2)
,
(m_{1},m_{2})=(10,1)
,
(m_{1})=(12)
.

(5)

in item
(2)
, if
s=4
, then for
g\in S
,
d_{g}\in\{1,2,3,4\}
. If
m_{i}=|g\in S,d_{g}=i|
, then the possible tuples
(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},m_{4})
are
(m_{1},m_{4})=(8,1)
,
(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})=(7,1,1)
,
(m_{1},m_{3})=(9,1)
,
(m_{2})=(6)
,
(m_{1},m_{2})=(2,5)
,
(m_{1},m_{2})=(4,4)
,
(m_{1},m_{2})=(6,3)
,
(m_{1},m_{2})=(8,2)
,
(m_{1},m_{2})=(10,1)
,
(m_{1})=(12)
.

(6)

in item
(7)
,
s\leq 6
.

(7)

in item
(7)
, if
s=6
, then
|S|=9
and
d_{g}=1
for all
g\in S
.

(8)

in item
(7)
, if
s=5
, then
d_{g}\in\{1,2\}
. If
m_{i}=|g\in S,d_{g}=i|
, then the possible tuples
(m_{1},m_{2})
are
(9,0)
and
(7,1)
.

(9)

in item
(7)
, if
s=4
, then
d_{g}\in\{1,2,3\}
. If
m_{i}=|g\in S,d_{g}=i|
, then the possible tuples
(m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})
are
(9,0,0)
,
(7,1,0)
,
(5,2,0)
,
(6,0,1)
.

Proof.

The proofs of all these statements come from the repeated use of
Proposition

5.4.5
, part
(2)
and
(3)
.
∎

Proposition 5.4.7
.

Suppose
m=2,k=1
and
D=9
. Let
S:=\{g\in G:d(g)>0\}
and
m_{i}=|g\in S,d_{g}=i|
. Then there do not exist admissible solutions for

(1)

s=6,(m_{1})=(9)

(2)

s=5,(m_{1})=(9)

(3)

s=5,(m_{1},m_{2})=(7,1)

(4)

s=4,(m_{1},m_{2})=(5,2)

(5)

s=4,(m_{1},m_{3})=(6,1)

Proof.

Proof of
(1)
Let
\widehat{d}
be the unnormalized Fourier transform

\widehat{d}(\chi)=\sum_{x\in G}d(x)\,(-1)^{\chi\cdot x}.

By
Lemma

5.1.1
, for every
\chi\neq 0
we have

\widehat{d}(0)-\widehat{d}(\chi)=4\ell(\chi).

Since
\widehat{d}(0)=\sum_{x}d(x)=D=9
, it follows that for
\chi\neq 0
,

\widehat{d}(\chi)=9-4\ell(\chi).

Moreover
\ell(\chi)=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{\chi\cdot x=1}d(x)
, and the sum on the right is at most

D=9
, hence
\ell(\chi)\leq 4
. Together with
\ell(\chi)\geq 2
, we conclude

\ell(\chi)\in\{2,3,4\}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\widehat{d}(\chi)\in\{1,-3,-7\}\quad(\chi\neq 0).

Let

n_{2}=\#\{\chi\neq 0:\ell(\chi)=2\},\quad n_{3}=\#\{\chi\neq 0:\ell(\chi)=3\},\quad n_{4}=\#\{\chi\neq 0:\ell(\chi)=4\}.

Then

n_{2}+n_{3}+n_{4}=63.

Step 1: first and second moment constraints.

Since
d(0)=0
, Lemma
5.1.3
gives the first moment constraint

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d}(\chi)=|G|\cdot d(0)=0.

Using
\widehat{d}(0)=9
, this becomes

9+\sum_{\chi\neq 0}\widehat{d}(\chi)=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad 9+(n_{2}-3n_{3}-7n_{4})=0,

i.e.

(33)

n_{2}-3n_{3}-7n_{4}=-9.

Next,
Lemma

5.1.5
yields

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}|\widehat{d}(\chi)|^{2}=|G|\sum_{x\in G}|d(x)|^{2}.

Here
|G|=64
and
d(x)^{2}=d(x)
since
d
is
\{0,1\}
–valued, so
\sum_{x}d(x)^{2}=D=9
.
Hence

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}|\widehat{d}(\chi)|^{2}=64\cdot 9=576.

Subtracting the
\chi=0
term (
|\widehat{d}(0)|^{2}=81
) gives

\sum_{\chi\neq 0}|\widehat{d}(\chi)|^{2}=576-81=495,

i.e.

(34)

n_{2}\cdot 1^{2}+n_{3}\cdot 3^{2}+n_{4}\cdot 7^{2}=n_{2}+9n_{3}+49n_{4}=495.

Solving the system consisting of

n_{2}+n_{3}+n_{4}=63,\qquad\eqref{eq:first-moment-ns},\qquad\eqref{eq:plancherel-ns},

gives

n_{4}=9,\qquad n_{3}=0,\qquad n_{2}=54.

In particular,

(35)

\widehat{d}(\chi)=\begin{cases}-7,&\text{for exactly }9\text{ characters }\chi\neq 0,\\
\ \ 1,&\text{for the remaining }54\text{ characters }\chi\neq 0,\end{cases}\qquad\text{and }\widehat{d}(0)=9.

Step 2: cubic moment contradiction.

Consider the triple convolution
d*d*d
and evaluate at
0
.
If
S=\{x\in G:d(x)=1\}
, then by
Lemma
5.1.6
(2),

(d*d*d)(0)=\#\{(x,y,z)\in S^{3}:\ x+y+z=0\}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

On the other hand, the cubic moment identity in the same lemma gives

(d*d*d)(0)=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d}(\chi)^{3}.

Using (
35
) and
|G|=64
, we compute

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d}(\chi)^{3}=9^{3}+54\cdot 1^{3}+9\cdot(-7)^{3}=729+54-3087=-2304.

Therefore

(d*d*d)(0)=\frac{-2304}{64}=-36,

which contradicts
(d*d*d)(0)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. This contradiction shows that no such
function
d
can exist.

Proof of
(2)
.
In this case, we have

(36)

n_{2}+n_{3}+n_{4}=31.

We have

\sum_{\chi\neq 0}\ell_{\chi}=2^{s-2}D.

Here
s=5
and
D=9
, so
\sum_{\chi\neq 0}\ell_{\chi}=2^{3}\cdot 9=72
, i.e.

(37)

2n_{2}+3n_{3}+4n_{4}=72.

Next apply Parseval:

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d}(\chi)^{2}=|G|\sum_{x\in G}d(x)^{2}.

Since
d\in\{0,1\}
, we have
\sum_{x}d(x)^{2}=\sum_{x}d(x)=9
, and
|G|=32
, so

\sum_{\chi}\widehat{d}(\chi)^{2}=32\cdot 9=288.

Subtracting the
\chi=0
term gives

\sum_{\chi\neq 0}\widehat{d}(\chi)^{2}=288-\widehat{d}(0)^{2}=288-81=207.

But for
\chi\neq 0
,
\widehat{d}(\chi)=9-4\ell_{\chi}
, hence

\widehat{d}(\chi)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\ell_{\chi}=2,\\
-3&\text{if }\ell_{\chi}=3,\\
-7&\text{if }\ell_{\chi}=4.\end{cases}

Therefore

(38)

n_{2}\cdot 1^{2}+n_{3}\cdot(-3)^{2}+n_{4}\cdot(-7)^{2}\;=\;n_{2}+9n_{3}+49n_{4}\;=\;207.

Solving (
36
), (
37
), (
38
) yields

(n_{2},n_{3},n_{4})=(24,4,3).

Indeed, from (
36
) and (
37
) we get
n_{3}+2n_{4}=10
, and substituting

n_{2}=31-n_{3}-n_{4}
into (
38
) gives
n_{3}+6n_{4}=22
, hence
n_{4}=3
,

n_{3}=4
, and
n_{2}=24
.

Finally, consider the triple convolution at
0
:

(d*d*d)(0)=\sum_{x+y+z=0}d(x)d(y)d(z)\ \geq\ 0,

since
d\geq 0
. On the other hand, Fourier theory gives

(d*d*d)(0)=\frac{1}{|G|}\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d}(\chi)^{3}.

Using
\widehat{d}(0)=9
and the multiplicities
(n_{2},n_{3},n_{4})=(24,4,3)
, we compute

\sum_{\chi}\widehat{d}(\chi)^{3}=9^{3}+24\cdot 1^{3}+4\cdot(-3)^{3}+3\cdot(-7)^{3}=729+24-108-1029=-384.

Thus

(d*d*d)(0)=\frac{-384}{32}=-12<0,

a contradiction. Hence no such function
d
exists.

Proof of
(3)
We have

(39)

n_{2}+n_{3}+n_{4}=31.

Using the standard first-moment identity

\sum_{\chi\neq 0}\ell_{\chi}=2^{s-2}D,

we obtain for
s=5
,
D=9
:

(40)

2n_{2}+3n_{3}+4n_{4}=72.

Subtracting
2\times
(
39
) from (
40
) gives

(41)

n_{3}+2n_{4}=10.

Now apply Parseval:

\sum_{\chi\in G^{*}}\widehat{d}(\chi)^{2}=|G|\sum_{g\in G}d(g)^{2}.

Here

\sum_{g}d(g)^{2}=7\cdot 1^{2}+1\cdot 2^{2}=11,

so

\sum_{\chi}\widehat{d}(\chi)^{2}=32\cdot 11=352.

Since
\widehat{d}(0)=9
, we get

\sum_{\chi\neq 0}\widehat{d}(\chi)^{2}=352-81=271.

But for
\chi\neq 0
,

\widehat{d}(\chi)=\begin{cases}1&\ell_{\chi}=2,\\
-3&\ell_{\chi}=3,\\
-7&\ell_{\chi}=4,\end{cases}

hence

(42)

n_{2}+9n_{3}+49n_{4}=271.

Substituting
n_{2}=31-n_{3}-n_{4}
into (
